FOREWORD
A PAR T from laying foundations of
demography in tbis subcontinent, a hundred
years of the Indian Census has also produced
'elaborate and scholarly accounts of the varie·
gated phenomena of Indian life-sometimes
with no statistics attached, but usually with
just enough statistics to give empirical underpinning to their conclusions.' In a country,
largely illiterate, where statistical or numerical
comprehension of even such a simple thing as
age was liable to be inaccurate, an understanding of the social structure was essential. It
was more necessary to attain a broad understanding of what was happening around one..
self than to wrap oneself up in 'statistical
ingenuity' or 'mathematical manipulation.'
This explains why the Indian Census came to
be interested in 'many by-paths' and 'nearly
every branch of scholarship, from anthropology and sociology to geography and religion.'
In the last few decades the Census has increasingly turned its efforts to the presentation
of village statistics. This suits the temper of
the times as well as our political and economic
structure. For even as we have a great deal of
centralization on the one hand and'decentralization on the other, my colleagues though it
would be a welcome continuation of the Census tradition to try to invest the dry bones of
village ·statistics with flesh-and·blood accounts
of social structure and social change. It was
accordingly decided to select a few villages in
every State for special study, where personal

v

VI

FOREWORD

observation would be brought to bear on the
interpretation of statistics to find out how
much of a village was static and yet changing
and how fast the winds of change were blowing and from where.
Randomness of selection was. therefore.
esc~ewed. There was no intention to build up

a pIcture for the whole State in quantitative
terms on the basis of villages selected statistically at random. The seleCtion was avowedly
purposive; the object being as' much to find
out what was happening and how fast to those
villages which had fewer reasons to choose
change and more to remain lodged in the past
a~ to discover how ~he more 'normal' types of
villa.ges were ch~nglllg .. They were to be primarily type studIes WhICh. by virtue of their
number and distribution, would also give the
reader a 'feel' of what was· going on and some
kind of a map of the country.
A brief .account of the tests of selection will
help to explain. A minimum of thirty-five
villages was to be chosen with great care to
represent adequately geographical, occupational and even ethnic diversity. Of this minimum of thirty-five, the distribution was to be
as follows:
(a) At least eight villages were to be so.
selected that each of them would contain one
dominaI?-t community with one predominating
occupatIOn, e.g. fishermen, forest workers
jhum cultivators, potters. weavers, salt-makers:
q~a~y workers etc. A village should have a
minlillum population of 400. the optimum
being between 500 and 700;
(b) At least seven villages were to be of
numerically prominent Scheduled Tribes of
the State. Each village could ·represent a particular tribe. The minimum population should
be 400, the optimum being between 500 and
700.
(c) The third group of villages should each
be of fair sjze, of an old and settled character
and _contain variegated occupations and be, if
~ossible. multi-ethnic in composition. By fair
SIze was meant a population of 500-700 persons or more ..The village should mainly
depend on agrIculture and be sufficiently
away from the major sources of modern communication such as the district administrative

headquarters and business centres. It should
be roughly a day's journey from the above
places. The ~ilI~ges. were t9 be selected with
an e.ye to vanatlOn m terms of size. proximity
to ~Ity and other means of modern communic~tIon, nearness to hills, jungles and major
nv~rs. Thus there was to be a regional distrib~tlOn throughout the State .of this category of
vI~lag~s .. If,. however, a partIcular district con~allled sIgmficant ecological variations within
ItS. area, more than one village in the district
mIght be selected to study the special adjustments to them.
It is a uniq~e feature of these village surveys
that they rapIdly outgrew their original terms
of ~eference, a~ my colleagues warmed up to
~helf work. ThIS proved for thein an absorbmg voyage of discovery and their infectious
en~h~slasm comp~lled me to enlarge the inqUIry s scope agalll and again. It was just as
well cautiously to feel one's way about at first
~nd then venture further afield, and although
It accounts to some extent for a certain unevenness in the quality and coverage of the
monographs, it served to compensate the
purely honorary and extra-mural rigours of
the .tas~. F?r, the survey, along with its many
ancIllanes lIke the survey of fairs and festivals,
of small and rural industry and others, was an
'extra', over and above the crushing load of
the 1961 Census.

It might be of interest to recount briefly the
stages by which the Survey enlarged: its scope.
At the first Census Conference in September
1959 the Survey set itself the task of what
might be called a record in situ of material
traits, like settlement patterns of the village;
house types; diet; dress; ornaments and footwear; furniture and storing vessels; common
means of transport of goods and passengers;
domestication of animals and birds; markets
attended; worsnip of deities, festivals and fairs.
There were to be re,cordings, of course, of cultural and social traits and occupational mobility. This was followed up in March 1960 by
two specimen schedules, one for each household, the other for the village as a whole,
which, apart from spelling out the mode of
inquiry suggested in the September 1959 conference, introduced groups of questions aimed
at sensing changes in attitude and behaviour
in such fields as marriage, inheritance, moveable and immoveable property, industry,

vn

fOREWORD

indebtedness, education, community life and
collective activity, social disabilities forums of
appeal over disputes, village leadership, and
organisation of cultural life. It was now plainly
the intention to provide adequate statistical
support to empirical 'fee1' to approach qualitative change thrqugh statistical quantities. It
had been difficult to give thought to the importal!ce of 'just enough statistics to give
empirical underpinning to conclusion', at a
time when my colleagues were straining themselves to the utmost for the success of the main
Census operations, but once the census count
itself was left behind in March, 1961, a series
of three regional seminars in Trivandrum
(May 19M), Darjeeling and Srinagar (June
1961) restored their attention to this field and
the importance of tracing social change
through a number of well-devised statistical
tables was once again recognised. This itself
presupposed a fresh survey of villages already
done~ but it was worth the trouble in view of
the possibilities that a 'Close analysis of statistics offered, and also because the 'consanguinity' schedule remained to be canvassed. By
Nuvember 1961,. however, more was expected
of these surveys than ever before. There w.as
dissatisfaction on the one hand with too many
general statements and a growing desire on the
other to draw conclusions from statistics, to
regard social and economic data as interrelated
processes, and finally to examine the social
and economic processes set in motion through

land reforms and other laws, legislative and
administrative measures, technological and
cultural c?ang~. Finally, a study camp
was orgamsed In the last week: of December
1961 when the whole field wat carefully 1T0ne
through over again and a programme wo~ked
out closely knitting the various aims of the
survey together. The social studies section of
the Census Commission rendered assistance to
State Superintendents by way of scrutiny and
technical comment on the frame of Survey
and presentation of results.
This gradual unfolding of the aims of the
Survey prevented my colleagues from adopting as many villages as they had originally
intended to. But I believe that what may have
been lost in quantity has been more than made
up for in quality. This is, perhaps, for the first
time that such a Survey has been conducted
in any country, and that purely as a labour of
love. It has succeeded in attaining what it set
·out to achieve; to construct a map of village
India's social structure. One hopes that the
volumes of this Survey will help to retain for
the Indian Census its title to 'the most fruitful
single source of information about the. country'. Apart from other features, it will perhaps
be conceded that the Survey has set up a new
Census standard in pictorial and graphic documentation. The schedules finally adopted for
this monograph have been printed in an ap.
pendix.

ASOK MITRA
Registrar General, India
.-oJ

New Delhi,
July 30, 1964.

VII!

PREFACE
The Reglstrar General's foreword gives a very clear idea of what the
project aims at achieving. For us it was not always very easy to select
villages where such surveys could be carried out. We inevitably collected
material for some of the places which have interested us and after a number
of considerations a selection was made.
Chergaon interested us because agricuiture and castes. polyandry and
trends are found here which are not common in other parts of Himachal
Pradesh. Then there is growing economy ~with elaborate potentialities. We
wanted to study the changes that have taken place here in the last decade
and a half or more.
This is not a snobbish reed but a brief monograph on socio-economic
survey of Chergaon about which we have gathered as much a vivid information of panorama of village life here as we possibly could. It takes years
to collect our material and to piece these together we have made a number
of initial mistakes. To bring out nearly 36 monographs of village surveys in
some of the remotest 'parts of Himachal Pradesh and to bring out another
large number of books means a considerable effort. patience. co-ordination
and devotion to work.
If we have been able to achieve this in some measure. what will stand
the test of time it would be because of the considerations that we have
received from Shri Asok Mitra, the Registrar General and from other
colleagues. Speaking of those from our organisation here, the initial
schedules have been very carefully and meticulously prepared by Durga
Singh our Office Superintendent. The Investigator, Mohan La! Gupta has
been to Chergaon many a times and has spent weeks together and much
longer. Some of us have been to· Chergaon several times. L. C. Sharma
and I have attended the Rohru fair at different times.

_
We have very vivid memories of these very beautiful and fascinating
'areas. As years passed this is no D).ore a remote area, the motor road and
'changing trends of agricultural economy are obvious. The soil seems to be
more hospitable. Right from Simla in October or, in autumn one can see the
IX

PREFACE

Irural way side echoing different prosperity for the present and the future.
There are tonnes of selected potatoes, apples and a bumper paddy crops at
the road side. Then hundreds of trucks go carrying apple. Areas from Rohru
in Chergaon and from lubbal onwards are very beautiful indeed for their
forests. Jubbal has been a very famous State in the past. All along these
areas some attractions or the other are found during the year.
Nature is lavish in the different times of year. When the road is ready,
Chergaon can attract probably ~ large number of tourists to visit these areas.
Rohru to Chergaon is an attractive walk. There are several kinds of trees,
Forest Nursery and trout fishing and these conjure for a delightful holiday.
Chergaon has a very comfortable Forest Rest House and is now receiving
more attention. There is a Block Development office which was recently
opened there'. More Government officials are visiting these areas and this is
a way to Dodra Kwar. Eld~ers have a sense of wit and humour they compare
to tlie areas in'which they live with whatever is more sophisticated they call
the Mall and the Lower Bazar o~ Simla. The women go to the fields to work
there with jewellery very often. Th,ey get together to celebrate fairs and
festivals. They are elegantly dressed 'in their own typical dresses and in their
own typical jewellery. We write in great deals the many things that have
interested us-the folk dances. Khushal Chand has been to many parts and
collected information on fairs and festivals and was there for the Bhoj festival.
I must say the photographs reveal an amazing peculiarities. One thing is absolutely certain that the type of horticulture that is indicating the changes,
would one day bring considerable prosperity to the peasants here. The change
of social customs and polyandry is there but the people have stuck to their
dresses, the typical and their belief for the village gods and it seems this will
not disappear soon.
I am grateful to Dr, B. K. Roy Burman, Officer on Special Duty
in the office of the Registrar Gen~ral, India, for going through the draft
report and his comments.
Most of the excellent photographs and drawings have been prepa1'led by
O.C. Handa. B.S. Thakur, Dharam Pal Sood, Mohan Lal Gupta and
L.C. Sharma have taken photographs and done arts work. Apart from these,
I have taken some pictures. The cover page photograph is an excellent study
taken of a group of ladies of a well-known family of Rohru Tehsil in their
"typical dresses and ornaments. This study was taken up by O.C. Handa at
the Hatkoti fair and shows very typical ornaments and dresses.
RAM CHANDRA PAL SINGH
BOSWELL SIMLA;

14 Nov. 1965

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
The study would probably not have been
complete, had the villagers not rendered proper
assistance to the investigator. We owe special
thanks to Shiv Saran a witty old mate, who
introduced the investigator to every villager.
He gave them an introductory talk about the
object of our survey work and has a fairly
wide knowledge of settlement history of
different castes. Sunder Singh Panchayat Secre~
tary and Thakur Lila Singh talked about the
agriculture. Kahan Singh tells all sorts of
stories about evil spirits and superstitions.
Gian Chand, weaver of Sonda is a master
craftsman. Master Parma Nand was kind
enough to give a number of folk songs.
Lastly, we are grateful to Sohan Lal, Magistrate 1st class who showed the old records
for Chergaon village. Devi Saran, the village
patwari is a fountain of information relating
to revenue. Hari Ram, B.D.O. of the newly
created block was helpful to the investigator.
R. K. Gupta, Tech. Asstt. of the agriculture
Department has kindly checked up the agriculture portion in Chapter III.
To all of them, we are extremely grateful.
RAM CHANDRA PAL SINGH

I. The~Village
introduction
Chergaon lies 9 miles fUrther away from
Rohru in the Chuhara valley. Chuhara is a
land of fertile fields and rich forests, A land
of gods. There are over one hundred devtas
in this valley. This village lies where Pabbar river and Andhra stream meet--Iocated
on the path to Dodra-Kawar, a remote corner
of the Tehsil. From Simla to Rohru, 80 miles,
one travels by the Himachal Pradesh Bus;
onto Chergaon is on foot. Once a while a jeep
pushes through. One passes through Seema,
Baidhyara, Mandli, Sondha and Bhonda villages.
At Seema and Baidhyara there are small
tea shops. There are two beautiful Fores~
log cabins. There is a beautiful forest Nursery on the right bank of Pabbar.
Willow plants remind us of, the beautiful
Kulu Valley or Kashmir.
Chergaon is divided in two parts. The firSti
consists of shops, small eating houses and
some houses. This portion lies scattered
@rQ'l,lnd the Andhra bridge.

Located here are the office of the Block
Development Officer, Trout Fisheries Development Farm, Government Middle School,
Primary Health Centre, Post Office, Forest
Rest House, Forest Nursery, Office of the
Deputy Range Officer, Gramsewak Hut and
Craft Centre. Some houses constructed in
the first part fall under Jangla Patwar Circle. This part is known as the Bhonda. These
are close to each other in a cluster. There is
shortage of accommodation.
The second part is the older portion of the
village inhabited by villagers on a hundred
yards uphill bridle path. Panchayat office and
a boarding house are situated in this part.
Patwar-khana is located in an isolated rented
house perched between the two parts of the
village.
Dominari~ castes in the village are Thakurs, Rajputs and Kolis. The mainstay of the
people is agriculture. Silversmithy, blacksmithy an:d weaving form subsidiary occupatiollS.
The village has aH area ot 281 acres and
• population of 352 persofi3.

THE ;vILLAGE

Origin of Chergaon

We rely on the history of the village as
available in revenue records. Chergaon is
about 400 years old and continues to expand.
The Hindi and English versions of the history, recorded in revenue records are reproduced. These throw light on the settlement of
Thakur families. The settlement history of
other castes have been given in the second
chapter.

English version
COpy OF THE STATEMENT MADE BY THE RESIDENTS OF VILLAGE CHERGAON DURING THE
LAST SETTLEMENT OF 1894.

Village-Chergaon.
Pargana-Surkhaligarh,
Bushahr State, Te4sil Rohru.
Clause I-Primary ownership rights and
division of land. First of all, Thakur of
Tikkri was the primary owner of this place.
When partition took place, Tikkri fell to the
share of one brother, this place fell to the
share 'of the other brother. After that pr~mary owners migrated from Tikkri and settled here. Some portion of this area was
inhabited and some unclaimed land was
brought under cultivation, and rest of the
land has been self acquired by the family.
Devta has land from the state to meet its
expenditure. The first partition was made of
the inherited property after the death of the
primary owners. After this, because uninhabited area was much, therefore unclaimed
land was reclaimed according to' one's needs
with due permission of the primary owner
and thus the holdings were increased. No
trace of original partjtioning can be found
and everybody is the recorded owner of the
land in his possession. :There is no common
land except pastures, 'inhabited area and
irrigation channels. There is no custom to
partition the pastoral land. The individuals
used to graze one's cattle in the aforesaid
pastures. In common land, there are certain
plots which are not in the possession of individua1 owners :and about these plots, only
those have been declared as owners who
possess the plots. If iIi common land, there
is some excess plot of pasture, that can be
reclaimed with due permission of the owner
or the State. From the said viUage 19 bighas
of land were deleted and added in Danwali
village circle. No hamlets have been included in the said village.
Clause II-How the village derived its
name. In early :;ztagesl wh~n there Wruj ,little

habitation, this place was known as Chergaon. Reasons for naming the village Chergaon are not known. When primary owners
settled in this village; the village they known
Chergaon and continues to be called so upto
this day. It has always remained inhabited
and was never deserted.

Clause III-The narration of the mode of
payment of Jand revenue.
In the early stages, this area belonged tQ
Thakur of Tikkri. We never paid any revenue, l;lecause the primary owners were the
descendants of Thakur of Tikkri. After that
when Thakur was killed by the ruler of
Bushehr State, the village and other surrounding area was merged with the state. At
that time, there was no fixed standard to
govern the payment of land revenue. It was
collected from time to time in accordance
with the whims and fancies of the ruler.
During those days the system of paying land
revenue in cash was not common. The payment was made mostly in kind and the
major payment used to be made in cereals,
oil and ghee. When expenditure of the State
exceeded its income in a particular year, we,
the land owners were responsible for sharing
the additional expenditure. The standard for
payment of land revenue in cash or in kind
including athada or other services to the ruling chief, was laid down in Sambat 1933 Bikrami. The land revenue was fixed according
to the size of the holdings. Now the ruler can
increase or decrease the land revenue of the
primary owners. Till today, if expenditure
exceeds income, we deposit the deficit by way
of cesses Bachh. The extra expenditure on
account of marriage and death are also met
by us in addition t9 .land revenue.

Physical Aspects
ChergaoJi is situated oil the ridge of Kalkapattam'mountains at a height of 6,000'. It is
in a semicircle· with vast fields stretched all
around. It lies on the 77°-53' E longitude
and 31 ° -14' N latitude. The village is '.Surrounded by Chausal, Buranghati, K~lkapattam and Murardanda, ranges, heIghts
var:ying between 8,000 feet to 15,000 feet.
Lower altitudes contain more sand brought
by Andhra and Pabber. Irrigation channels
drawn from Andhra continue to feed the
strips of land at lower levels. Early settlers
had planted kunesh trees along Andhra
s~ream. Th,esE; trees give fuel and fodder,

stone, quartzite, shale stone and schists.
Shale stone is used for the construction of
wall and as slates for house roofing.

Climate and Rainfall
The surroquding hills remain snow-covereo ior more 't'nan six months ':m a -year.

Winter is chilly. $now continues upto
January and February. The average snowfall is two and a half feet. The heaviest
snowfall was during 1959-60 winter which
WaS more than six feet. The climate is cold.
Sweeping cold winds blow from the valleys.

Sparrow
Quail

In summer, the- cLimate is wanm~r. The
pleasant seasons are Spring and Autumn.
These are mild and brading. During rains
most of the rain falls during July, August
and September. Little rain also falls during
winter. Average yearly rainfall is 35". 1947
was the worst year when it rained too much.
There were floods in Pabber and Andhra.
The Andhra bridge, two flour grinding mills
and low-lying fields on the banks of the
stream were washed away.

Reptiles
Chameleon
Cobra
Snake

years in 'respect of Rohr'll" indicates peak
rainy months of the s~ason-July, August
and September.

Residential Pattern
The houses are built in any place that is
available. We see some white-washed houses
on both sides of the road. In the main village, a number of closely ~uilt houses are
there on the left. On the rIght, the houses
are not so dense. Above there are narrow
streets which converge on at different points
upto main path. One can pass through these.
There are no drains and water stagnates.
Below the path the portion is a bit cleaner.
The scheduled castes have separate settlements. They have built their houses in both
portions of the main viHage. But three of
. them have built their houses adjacent to the
houses of the swarns.
The houses are double storeyed and more
or less identical. The houses of swarns and
harijans have one difference. A:- swarn's
house is generally more commodlOus and
Mgger than a harijan's house. The houses
have slate roofs. An elder introduces his
portion of Chergaon as the 'Lower Bazar'.

Cremation Ground

The dead are cremated on the bank of
Andhra stream near the confluence of the
stream with Pabber. This is sli"ghtly away'
from the vil~age. There are three separate
burning places used by Rajputs, Thakurs and
Kolis. The burning-ghat is known as marvi.
Market

Chergaon is a market for dhoop and medicinal herbs such as k,q,TOO and kuth. Some
shops are run by Suds who. come from
Kangra. More than 2,000 maunds of raw
dhoop and roo maunds of other medicinal
herbs were marketed every year. Now the
quantity has been reduced. These are purchased by shopkeepers who have opened

THE VILLAGl<!

their shops in the interior of Tikkral and
Jugha Valleys. The main production centres
of these raw materials are Rohal, Pekha,
Deodi, Jakha and J anglik. Herbs are collected from peaks. Men, women and children
bring these to Chergaon. They are sold to
shopkeepers. In exchange they buy commodities of daily use, which include cloth, salt,
tobacco and gUT. This barter system goes on
till midnight and after a little nap, the
energetic villagers march back to their
villages in the morning. The villagers dispose of dhoop at cheap rates. In exchange
they buy goods at exorbitant rates. Shopkeepers get this pTofit. Raw dhoop is purchased by shopkeepers at Rs. 20 to Rs. 25 per
maund. After drying, it is exported to Delhi
and Amritsar where it fetches approximately Rs. 100 per maund. The busy season for
dhoop is from early June and continues up to
the end of October.

II. The People, their Culture
and Material Equipment
The People

Apart from Rajputs, Kolis, Lohars and
Badhis reside here. There are 8 households
of Brahmans, seven are government servants and one is a shopkeeper. There is one
Khatri family. Two families are Suds. They
have come from outside. Rajputs deserve a
particular mention. Out of 40 Rajput households, 11 call themselves Thakurs. They
consider superior to other Rajputs proper as
descendants of Thakurs who ruled this area
long ago.

We give .a brief note on the main castes in
the village.
Thakurs-Thakurs are the first settlers
and regard' themselves superior to others.
There are 11 households consisting of 30
males and 28 females. According to Shiv

Saran mate, the Thakurs settled three centuries ago. The present Jabbal devta of Rohru
Tehsil had his native place near Sangla in
Kinnaur.· The devta came over to Chansal
peak via Buran-Ghati searching for a place
to settle in. After resting for several days,
he proceeded to lower Koti. The five Mahasu
devtas did not Like his coming. They offered
resistance'. J abbal dev<ta !had to retreat to
Chansal peak. Then the devta followed the
path in Tikkral Valley and reached Tikkri
ruled by a powerful Thakur family. The
elder brother was infirm and powers were
exercised by the younger brother. Chergaon
was under the control of the Thakur of
Tikkri. When the devta arrived in Tikkri in
disguise, the two brothers were busy merry
making with their companions. The party
consisted of eighteen persons and each had
a bottle of wine and plenty of meat. Before
the party began, one goat for each member
was slain by the order of the younger
Thakur and the elder brother was asked to
clean the inside of these goats. When the
elder Thakur was cleaning these along the
river, a vulture picked up one stomach and
ate it. This was the devta in disguise. The
Thakur took the remaining potis to his
younger brother, who was dead drunk. At
the sight of 17 pieces of stomach,. he: ~elt
insulted and passed cruel remarks InSIsting
on his finding the missing poti. The other
participants of the party intervened arid
requested the younger Thakur not to insult
the elder· brother in the presence of the
gathering. He just did not care. The elder

l(}

a:SE PEOPLE, THEIR CULTURE AND MATERIAL EQUIPMENT

brother came weeping to the river and sat
with his hands on the head. The devta was
observing all. He took pity and appeared
before him in the guise of a sadhu. The
story was narrated. The sadhu consoled him.
He took a bow and an arrow and shot down
a vulture and gave the stomach to the
Thakur and directed him to take it to his
younger brother.
The 18th stomach was produced but the
younger brother insisted in his leaving
Tikkri at once and wanting him to settle
down in Chergaon. This waS-his share of the
property. The elder Thakur brother came to
Chergaon with his wife. The 'devta also
accompanied them and stayed th'er~ for a
couple of weeks. The devta and the Th?kur
now became dharam bhai by tying thr'ead
on each other's fore-arms. Where the devta
stayed is now marked by a raised stone .
platform. This stands in the middle of the
fields. After' a brief stay, the devta again
moved to a _pltace in Jangla Valley. During
wandering he frequently visited his dharam
bhai, the Thakur of Chergaon and was
worried as he had no children. At that time
J abbal village was ruled by Lakkan Pal
devta. He resented the interference from
another devta. These devtas had a duel.
Lakkan Pal devta broke his leg and ran
away. The devta from Kinnaur found a
secure place for his stay in J abbal village.
After firmly establishing himself, the
J abbal devta visited the house of a Thakur
in Koti. He found a group of persons drinking in the house. The devta cast a charm
and except the Thakur, his wife' and son,
killed others in the party. When the Thakur
and his son came to their senses, they chased the devta with an axe in their hands.
They had gone a little distance. Both of
them slipped into the Andhra khad and
were drowned.
To commemorate this
event, people throw thin pieces of wood
where they were drowned. The devta took
the widow of the deceased. She was married
to the Thakur of Chergaon and they had
children. The families of Thakurs claim to
be their descendants. The relation between
Thakur families of Chergaon and J abbal
devta are cordial. Some of the high-lights of
relations between the devta and Thakur
families represented by Thakur Lila Singh
ar~:-

1. Whenever an oracle of J abbal devta

dies, Thakur Lila Sin(!h becomes
sadhu for a day and full mourning
is observed in his house.
2. If there is some death in Lila Singh's
house, devta is' pot worshipped for

a day as he observes mournings for
the death.
3. When ;there is some marriage in the
family, of Thakur Li]a Singh, one
goat is slain in the name of the
Jabbal devta.
4. Whenever a devta on its visit to
some other village is offered a goat,
the skin and stomach of the goat is
,
always sent to Thakur Lila Singh.
5. The nazarana of Rs. 2 given by
Khabbal villagers to Jubbal devtafor grazing rights on pastures on
Kalka-pattam mountain are given
to Thakur Lila Singh by the devta
because in the past, Kalka-patltam
mountain was of Thakur's domain.
Thakurs are agriculturists and till their
own fields. They are well off and form the
elite of the village. Both polygamous and
polyandrous types of marriages are allowed
in This community. Widow marriage is permitted. In this commuility marriages are held
in the same group. Preference is however
given to the marriages in the village nearer
to Chergaon. They spend more on marriag~s
as compared with others on account of theIr
high social position in the village. They have
a 'separate place where they cremate the
deads.
Rajputs-There are 19 households of Rajputs. Here Rajputs are ranked 2nd to Th,akurs, but enjoy equal and same cultural
standing with them, The elderly persons
revealed that they have migrated from Kinnaur. Shiv Saran, an elderly member of this
community felt that about 3 generations
back, a Kinnaur family came to MandJi, ,a village 3 miles away, ~ and were engaged by one
Waziroo a landlord of the village. As the
family increased the younger ones moved
towards Chergaon in search of more land,
where they could live and earn their livelihood. To s~art with, some land was purchased from the Th,akur of Chergaon and more
was added later on.
Ohe household of Rajput has migrated
from Atharabis, in Rampur Tehsil and another household from Havchadi, a village 6
miles away. These are the cases of recent
migration:, and settlement. . Two familie~
have migrated from Bhalyam and Dhangoh
villages.
Most of the Rajput families like sa'ltish
tea very common in Kinnaur. The Banias
of the village address these Rajputs as Negi
commonly used for the people of Kinnaur.
The Rajputs of the village frankly admit

THE PEOPLE,

THEIR

CULTURE AND

MATERIAL EQUIPMENT

that origin of their anc:e9'tors was in Kinnaur. If one happens to see Deva Nand a
Rajput of the village, the first impression is
that he is a Kinnaura. He is a migratory from
the upper part of Bushahr State.
People of this caste are strong and sturdy
and are mostly busy in their agricultural
workl. ThiS! COminunity IS neither rich nor
poor. Their social and other aspects of life
are similar to those of Rajputs living in Chuhara.
Kolis-There are 14 families of Kolis. The
families have migrated from Chalala,
Bhonda, Khanyara, - Chatoli, Tangnoo and
Riyaloo villages situated around Chergaon
within a radius of 10 to 14 miles. One
family has migrated from Kinnaur and
another from Garhwal. Some feel they
migrated for economic reasons and there is
another class of thinkers who feel that Kolis
from the nearby different villages were sent
for to plough the ,agricultural land of the
Thakur of Chergaon. Out of the total 14
families of Kolis, only 5 ,families own land
and the others are working as tenants to
rich Thakur families. They seem to be in a
sorry' plight and are being exploited by the
upper class.
Ap.art from being tenants, they work on
roads and buildings. Sometimes they work
on daily wages with the Forest Department.
They do not marry in Lohar or Badhi
castes. Widow re-marriage is allowed and
polyandrous and polygamous marriages are
common among Kolis.
Lohar-There are two households of this
community. They have no house or land.
They have migrated from Tiplu and Tangnoo villages. One household works on
bellows and 'lives in a rented house belonging to a Thakur. The rent is in kind and labour. He gives three iron ploughs free to the
Thakur and also works on his fields. He had
certain duties to perform for the benefit of
the village community. H~ makes or mends
iron agricultural implements. The wages
are insufficient for him. Half the time he
works in another village and supplements
his income.
The second Lohar is a goldsmith. He has
come from Tangnoo in: Dodra Kawar and
has no landed property in ChergaoIi. He
manages to make both ends meet by making
ornaments. His wages are in cash and iIi
kind. He also lives in a rented house belonging to a Thakur. In lieu of rent he does no~
charge anything from the Thakur for the
~rvices rendered.

11

Badhi-There is one family of Badhi. He
is the village carpenter-cum-mason. His forefathers had come from Chaloti, five miles
away. Besides making and mending wooden
agricultural tools, he is an agricul~urist. This
gives little )ncome. For making agricultural
tools, he gets customary share from the produce of the two harvests. He is equal to a
Lohar in social status. He is not allowed to
enter the house of Rajput or Thakur except
when he is to carry out some rep'airs and
alteration inside the house. Then he may
enter with a tool in his hand. He is engaged
dn constructing houses. He receives wages
in kind. Banias and Thakur families sometimes' pay his wages in cash. He is also an
expert in erecting stone walls.
House Type
Houses are designed on the traditional
pattern found in Chuhara Valley. These are
pacca or kacha. Both types have slate roof.
The houses are double storeyed with a small
room pand in between the two storeys.
These exclude houses or building belonging
to Government institutions.
The prevalent rates for the different items
of material required for the house construction are as:Stone from quarry1. Stones-Rs. 5 as a permit fee for nonright holders or contractors. Free for
right holders.
2. Slates-Rs. 5 for non-right holders or
contractors. Free to right holder villagers.
Timber-Zamindari rates (right holders) of
different classe~ and species from Rs. 2
to Rs. 5. Market rates (Non-right holders) of different classes and species
from Rs. 2 to Rs. 900.
In case zamindar cuts timber without a
pennit, he is fined to the tune of the zami.fi.dari rate of that tree plus compensation rate
of the tree which ranges from Rs. 2 to
Rs. 100. This depends on the girth and
quality of timber. In case any non-right
holder is caught stealing timber, he is fined
the market rate of that tree plus a penalty
of Rs. 50. The villagers have timber rights
in Punjali, Bobla and Sa:ndasu forest ranges
within a radius of 3 to 6 miles. Stones and
slates are available at Punjali quarry three
miles away. The present rate of a stone
chhatta of 5'x5'x4' is Rs. 10 at the quarry.
These are carried at 'the owner's expense.
Nuts and bolts are bought from local shopkeepers.

THE PEOPLE;

THEIR

CULTURE AND

MATERIAL EQUIPMENT

Trees are obtained by applying to the
Forest Department through the Panchayat.
The quantity of material required is usually
recommended by the Panchayat.
Labowr-Prior to 1947-48, stones and
wood used to be collected by all villagers
and the owner used to give meals to them.
This was not a forced labour. By this the
work was done' quickly in a collective form.
At the site of the stone quarry or the place
from where timber was brought, the work
was done by the owner at cheap rates. The
masons and carpenters were working on a
contract. The charges for construction are
agreedl, before the work starts. The mason
used to charge Rs. 20 for a one-storeyed
house and Rs. 35 for a double storeyed
house.
Now labour is employed on daily wages.
The rates are:Mason
Rs. 4.00
Carpenter
Rs.4.00
Labourer
Rs. 2.00
In some cases payment is made in kind. A
mason or a carpenter is given two pathas of
grains whereas a labourer is given one patha
of grains. These are in addition to food once
a day. One patha contains 1 seer of paddy or ~
seer of barley. Only rich families of Thakurs
or Rajputs afford to give wages in kind.
Mah1Lrat--When: a new hOllile has to be
constructed, a mahurat is sought from the
family priest of Dhangoli. There are no
pa:ndits in Chergaon. Sometimes, the family
detJta is consulted for the mahurat. Navratras
falling in Chait are preferred. There is little
rain and the villagers are free.
Foundation-While laying the foundation
of a house, gur is distributed among those
gathered. For foundation two feet deep and
H feet wide trenches are dug. These are
watered for days. Four stones are kept at the
corners of the trenches. This marks the beginning of work. A rupee is given to the mason.
There are many superstitions for completing the foundation upto the ground level.
The owner of a close relative, under the
evil effect of say Saturn should quit the site
till the foundation is laid. A constant vigil is
kept. This is done to avoid any possibility of
throwing a charm. The charm may consist
of hair, nails and ash wrapped in red cloth.
When old houses were pulled down, inauspicious articles like 'these were found in the
walls. A vigilance is kept at nights.
Construction-Generally no plinths are
bui'lt. The terrain is not flat. Walls are about
I! feet wide m.ade OF stone and: bea~ of

13

wood. No mud mortar is used. When the door
frames are fixed, g1Lr is again distributed
among the labourers, near relatives and
friends. The first storey is about 6 feet or 7
feet high. The stones used in the construction
of the house are of a variety of shale. These
are rectangular and uniform. Wood is used
in the walls. A wooden beam 9 inches thick
is used and then again stone work starts. This
is to make the house durable and to keep it
warm during winter. Dressed stones are kept
on both sides of the wall. In between the
gaps, small uneven stones are filled. Sometimes, the wall is made of dressed stones.
Door-The doors made of diar wood are
about
x 42' in dimension. The door frame
is thick. It is made of one piece of wood about
2 inches to 3 inches thick. The doors are
quite low. These are provided with a latch
opened from outside.
The door handle or "locks are ornate. Many
long curved keys are used. The main door of
the house need not face in any particular
direction. This depends on the house site.
The door facing east is considered better.
Second Storey-After the ground floor is
completed, work starts on the second storey.
The floor is made of diar planks,
The ground floor and the first storey are
called goshal and bawad. In goshal cattle are
kept while bawad is a room used as sleeping
room, kitchen, store all into one.
Pand-In some houses, a small space between the ground floor and first storey is provided. This is covered from all sides and is
only about four feet high. Entrance to thi!':
room lo~ally called pand is through a small
trap door in the floor of the bawad. Pand is
a closed, dark and dingy sort of room and has
no windows or ventilators. The family retires
to it in winter months because of the cold
weather. Few houses are provided with pando

3r

14

THE PEOPLE,

Roof-The gabled roof is laid over a structure of wooden beams. The roof material
is slate. A new Government Primary School
building has G.!. sheet roof. One build.i!).g of
the Fisheries Department and one hut of the
Forest Rest House have roofs of wood. The
Forest Rest House's roof is made of, wooden
planks and part slates.
Tong-A wooden verandah known as tong
extends into three or four directions is also
constructed. For some Rajput and Thakur
families, toogs are -covered with wood planks
carved. These have small wind<,?w.
Finishing touch-After completion of the
construction work walls are plastered with
mud from inside. The inner walls are given
a coat of shukla mata. This is a form of
white-washing.
Kothar-Every house must have a store
room called kothar or kotha in the 10cal
dialect. Kothar is constructed close to the
house. In some cases it is constructed in the
ground floor next to the goshal. In kothar
there are four big compartments, for storing
grains. Other food stuffs are stocked in small
kothaTies. The big
compartment is
ten feet to fifteen feet in depth and 2' x 2' in
dimension. Kothar is kept locked and the key
is with an elder of the family. A separate
kothar has given to keep grains safe from
fire and foodgrains may be saved.
Kitchen-Few have separate kitchen. An
opening is made in the roof. In rain or sn()w,
a stone is placed. The walls and the ceilings
get sooty. These rooms have no windows but
only small doors. The rooms are dark and inside is stuffy.
Bath-There are few baths or latrines
attached to houses. The shopkeepers or
Government building may have these facilities. The villagers bathe either in verandah

or in th open. In summer, women bathe
under the trees by the flowing kuhl. They do
not bathe too often in winter. Whenever they
do, it is with warm water at night in the
open. Table No.5 gives households by r.um.
ber of. rooms and by number of person<;

Household Goods
One finds a chair, table and charpoy in
Lila Singh's house or with the Panchayat
Secretary Sunder Singh. Mostly others do not
own furniture. Govt. officials and shopkeepers possess some. Villagers sleep on the
floor. Thakatposh is there in some houses.
These are fixed in the tong.
Tiny kerosene wick lamps are common in
every household. A few use lanterns. Shopkeepers have Petromax lamps. The other
goods are sit and olant~ made of stone for
grindin,g, shoop for winnowing, kilta for
carrying things. For spinning there is takdi,
kindaklu and kOTU. Wool is kept in a kandi.
In many households of Kolis, chari a big

wooden log is kept for storing water. It is
kept covered in the verandah. Hollow logs
are kept for the cattle in goshal. These are
known as kharal and kunaZta that contain
fodder and water. The rug used is called
fakula aT kharch:a made of goat's hair. For
measuring grains, patha is used. Patha is of
different capacity. The hukka and pipe with
a chilam is a must there. Now some use
narial made of hard coconut instead of the
hukka. This is cheap and easy to move about
around. Wooden boxes known as kothi are
found in some houses. In every h~use,
there are about 3~4 brooms known as shooni.
There are 8 radio sets. Except the Panchayat radio these are with Government servants or shopkeepers. Three households have
guns and sewing machines.

Earthen pots are purchased from potters in
Jhat WaH four miles away.' These -are
brought from potters who move around and
come from Mandl and Bilaspur.- Sometimes

grains are given for pots_ A barter deal takes
place. The roving potters move from one vil.
lage to another village, they sell pots by making them on the spot.

Metal utensils are bought from local shopkeepers. The water is fetched in tokani
carried in kiLta. Aluminium utensils are not
used. A queer type of wooden prat known as
kunalta is used, by a few.
Cups, saucers and plates are used by som7·
A big hollow w:ooden log, known as goo too 1S
kept near the spring. Women wash there
the clothes.
Dress
The men dress like Kinnauras.
Tota-It is the famous Bushahri cap. It is a
woollen cap with the velvet portion of a cloth
turned upwards. The flap is let down in cold
weather to protect the ears. Before 1947 Kolis
were not allowed to use the velvet band in
their caps. If anyone was found using it, he
could be reprimanded.
Kurta-It is a 'cotton shirt with or without
collars and a pocket on the above left side.
Sadri-It is a cotton or woollen waistcoat.
Jurkhi-It is a woonen aclikan upto the
knees. Sometimes during winter, men are
seen putting two woollen jurkhis. This hs.s
no lining.
Gachhi-It is a few men use gachhi. It is
about 4 to 5 yards long and is generally dark
,
red -in colour . .It is tied round the waist.
Suthan-It is a pyjama made of cotton or'
woollen cloth and is narrow at the bottom
sometimes richly embroidered known as
Khamarudar.
DRESS FOR WOMEN

Dhattoo-Two types of dhattoo are put on
by women as head dress. The first type and
the most traditional one is made of two parts.
The 1st part is called topi, a round cap of
about nine inches and the 2nd part is the
talla. A big white piece of cloth is folded
four times. This becomes about 2t' x I'. The
topi is put on, then taLla is wrapped round
the head and tied to one side. The smooth side
comes over the forehead. This appears to be
a peculiar head dress. A favourite' with old
woman. The 2nd type of dhattoo is a one yard
square coloured cloth. This is folded .twice
with corners together. It forms a trIangle
and then is placed over the head. The two
farther ends are given a simple knot at the
back of the neck. Young women do not use
a two piece dhattoo. Instead they put on one
piece dhattoo.
Kurta-It is a cotton shirt known as jumper. A grey miletia cloth is quite commonly
used for kurta.
Sadri-During winter few women use
woollen waistcoat inside the jurkhi, it is
called sadri.

17

Jurkhi-It is a woollen achkan. Sometimes
this has upturned sleeves with red silk lining
and hem. During summer, cotton jurkhis
are used.
Gochhi-Women wrap a piece of cotton or
woollen cloth around their waist. This i<;
about fifteen feet long. A shade of mauve
colour.
Suthan-It is a woollen pyjama of grey or
brown shades.
Salwar-Some young women put on cotton
salwar.
Rebdar suthan-It is a cotton pyjama, tight
at the bottom.
Othmo-Old women
wear traditional
woollen half pyjama upto the knees.
Hair style-Young men like what they call
the English hair cut. Some old men keep long
hair round their head but remove them over
the scalp. Women keep them plaited on both
sides and tying these plaits at the end with
a black cotton jutti. Mustard or apricot oil
is the hair oil.
Foot wear-These are made by a local shoemaker. He migrated from Ambala District. .
Another shoe-maker was given a machine
by the Development Department. He has left
and gone. Young men and school going
children use canvas shoes. Lawatey are the
hand made shoes. Tliese are becoming rare.
Slippers or sandals are used by house wives
of shopkeepers or Government Officials.
Ceremonial Dress-Slight change is observed on ceremonial occasions as far as the
dress is concerned. On fairs and festivals, the
most liked colours among women are black,
yellow or pink. On such occasions some put
on new Bushahri caps. Men may use khambroodar suthan. The upper portion is of usual
woollen cloth. The lower part below the
knees, is made of different multicoloured
designs. This is woven in very colourful
designs.
Change in dress-The younger people
prefer coloured striped pyjama. Pullovers
are replacing sadris. The famous Bushahri
cap still holds its own. Mill made cloth is
preferred for the kurta. Among women
,there is a slight tendency for a change. They
have started putting on cotton suthan and
salwars.
Tailoring and Cost of Cloth-Costly clothes
are not used as a form of daily attire. Dress
are stitched by two tailors of the viilage.
Government officlals and shopkeepers get
their clothes stitched from tailors at Rohru.

THE PEOPLE,

Superstitions-There are some :mperstiHons about the dresses. Sunday is considered

inauspicious for stitching or wearing new
clothes. If a dead fly is found while clothes
are being stitched this is considered a bad
omen. Whoever puts on these clothes would
fall ill.
Food and Drink
The diet comprises of barley, rice and
wheat. Maize is also liked. Barley and wheat
are taken from April ,to October. l\_ice and
maize are consumed during winter. Be!lides,
millets like koda, chulaie, kawani, faf ra, ogta,
bathoo china are. also in the menu. Tlie,
grains ~re ground separately into flour. These
are kneaded with water. Bread is baked and
known as roti. Rice is relished. Shathi variety
of rice grown in the Tehsil has a flavo~~known for its taste it is sought for in a~Joming areas.
Pulses and Vegetables-With roti and rice,
pulses form a part of the menu. Mash, moong
and masar varieties of pulses are grown.
These are relished by ,all. Vegetables are taken
throughout the year. These are used n:?re
during monsoon5 when green vegetades luu:,
pumpkin, cabbage, lingad, ghiya, are !>~own.
So are vegetables like potatoes, arbt ~nd
kachalu grown. These are consumed dUrlllg
winter generally. A variety of mushroomchhattri and chhaoon are consumed. The
green leaves of fafra and chabroo are boiled
and fried in m\lstard oil and then served..
Sattu-Sdtt~ made
of . barley taken
throughout the year. It is prepared in the
month of JaftTi from grains of barley. Sometimes half ripe barley Is ground. In other
cases ripe, barley is use~ .to pr~pare s.attu.
This is consumed by mlxmg WIth lasst . or
gur., Poorer families take this preparatlOn
with salt and water.
Changush-There is one preparation made
from a kind of root known as changush found
in jungles at higher altitudes. This is kept
soaked in water of a spring for 3-4 days so
that its bitter taste washes away. Then this
is boiled and taken with salt. One precaution
is that this preparation is not chewed. It is
simply gulped. If this p~epardtioIi i~ chewe~,
lips and tongue get swollen. ThIS food IS
taken by poorer people.
Fruits-From June to October apncot,
peach and pears are the common fruits.
Few households have planted apple trees.
Beverages-Tea is takw once a ddy by
many households. There was a belief in some
households of Rajputs and Ihakurs that tneir
devta had imposed a ban on the use of milk.
Therefore, they convert milk into ghee and
lassi. llut this belief is no longer followed

strictly. Households possessing buffaloes sell
milk to the Banias and to Govcrn.llf:nt offlcials.
Special preparati.an-B'€sides the usual diet
some special dishes are also prepared on slJe'
cial occasions or ceremomes. 'Ihesl: <.tre poidu
bada and shakudai, fried in mustard oi{
Poldu are made of ferment<::d flour and iried
in oil, whereas bada are made of ground
mash-pulse and then fried in the oil. Shakdi
is made of rice. First rice is ground into
flour and is mixed with wat~r to ~orm a paste.
From it, very thin chapaties are steam-baked
and dried. These are then fried iIi the oil
Sweat fried roties are dl::;o made dnd afe
.known as chooday. These are like puris in
the. plains. Khir and hallca are prepan~d by
RaJputs and Thakurs on occasions like marriages or when there is a birth of a c~ild in the
f~mily. At marriage of an elder person in a
nch Thakur family wheat flour is bailed with
\\-ater. This is known as badi and is given to
guests with gUT and ghee.
Meat-Goat's meat is known as dalki. Dry
meat is taken and its preparation is very
Simple. Dalki is boiled in water ar.d then fi'ied in oil or ghee. About a de<'ade back, taidng
of eggs and fowls was tabooed in certain
families of Thakurs and Rajputs. But :'10\~'
eggs are common with all, wlth a few th<! old
taboo remains. Meat of wild animals like
ghoral and kakar is relished, by the villagers. These animals are found in the nearbv
dense forests. Fish is liked as Pabbar and
Andhra are full of them.
Wine-Wine is taken by almost every
elderly person iIi each family. The common
wine is a local one made from fafra and koda
grains. Barley, gur and baimi are also used
in distilling wine. To distil wine, thick roties
of fafra or koda as the case may be are kept
with hot water for 12-15 days in a wooden
box known as kothi. Then some flour of
bithoo or a specia,l powder known as keem is
mixed and is allowed to ferment for threefour days. When the mixture gets ready, it is
disti'lled and wine obtained and used. The
people are so addicted to wine that the local
production does not meet their requirement.
A country wine vend shop locally called Lhari
has been opened in the village.
Smoking-Men ~moke hukka or cigarettes.
Among women, only old ones arc habitual
smokers. People here have a novel method
of smoking tobacco while on journeys or
grazing their cattle. They dig <i small hole
in the ground and put tobacco and fire over
it. This hole is linked undergrourrd with
another hole about nine inches away through
which they smoke tobacco using both hands.

So far as fats are concerned, only vegetable ghee, mustard oil, al)d goat's fats are
used for preparing the daily diet. Desi ghee
is seldom used. The villagers use the oil
extracted from the kernels of apricot nuts.
In their daily intake, fruits form the negligible percentage except in the summer
months. The consumption of other protective
food like milk, eggs and ghee is not adequate.
33 households take 2 meals a day, :34 households take 3 meals a day. A Thakur family
takes four meals a day. There are three vegetarian households in the village, two of them
are Govt. officials and the third is a Sud
shopkeeper. Eighteen households do not take
sugar and fourteen households are not in the
habit of taking tea. The majority of such
households comprise Harijan families. Castewise data on food habits are given in tables
8-10.
Fue~ and Lighting-Chullah is the old type
of hearth still in use. It is made in a eorner
of the room in such a way that to one side of
the chullah, the house-wife can easily sit
while cooking meals. In pand, one more type
of chullah is constructed in the centre
known as koond or chool. The food over
koond is cooked· by keeping a lohadan. This
is an iron tripod. Cowdung and mud plaste:'
is applied to the. hearth at regulp..r mtervals
so as to ~eep it clean. An outlet in the roof
above the hearth, known as sarag duar is
made for smoke outlet. Chlllli. a1'1l, cheeI,
kayal and Iounesh trees furnish th,e fuel.
Dried cowdung known as goshtay is uj>ed
during winter. Its fire remains for long.
The village is not electrified at present
though Rohru is electrified. Lan terns and big
lamps are used by the Government officials

and shopkeepers. Tiny kerosene wick lamps
are used by a few villagers. In many households, thin long pieces of kayaI wood is used
for lighting. It is very economical and serves
the purpose of kerosene lamp. Its thin strips
are known as jokMies and are ournt. in a tin
stand hung from the roof known as
hoshiaroo.
Only richer nouseholds use match box.
Traditional way of striking a flame is still
common. They strike a piece of irorz-achkon
with a white storze-shaiyot on O'1e side a
dried grass-bachaya is closely kept. The
bachaya catches the sparks from the tinder
and the fire is produced by constant blowing
for ~ few seconds . .Achkon is made locally
by VIllage blacksmIth and shai-yot is also
available on the banks of the stream.
Bachaya is found over high peaks.

Ornaments
Every husband likes his wife to possess
important items of ornaments to be used
on fairs, festivals or during ceremonies.
He feels crestfallen if his wife does not
possess a few. Sometimes he takes the step
of mortgaging hi's land or landing himself
into debts.
In the hills prior to independence, the
Kolis were not allowed to put on gold ornaments. The elderly villagers told that it was
partly due to respect which the Thakurs and
Rajputs commanded and partly due to the
shattered economy of the Kolis. Now the time
has changed, and the women of the castes
have started using gold ornaments.
We find the tendency among young girls
and young women to use lighter ornaments

21

place of the old and heavy o:Q,es. The investigator observed in some of the fairs this
change very distinctly. The old women are
,not going ~o part with their old fashioned
and 'heavy ornaments. In some families
heavy and old ornaments are being moulded into light ornaments. The choice of their
young daughters or daughters-in-law. After
the gold control order, we do not find much
change regarding wearing of gold ornaments
as has been observed in villages round Ko~khai. There many women have almost ceased
wearing gold, ornaments and use many simplest and cheapest ornaments.
.
Goldsmith of the village makes map.y ornaments. Some get their ornaments made by
the goldsmiths living in Machhli and Rohal
villages. Silver or gold used to be :taken to the
goldsmith and_ the artisan ,made 0!llaments of
their choice. It is rare that a VIllager buys
ready made ornaments.
Some common ornaments are:ORNAMENTS FOR THE HEAD

Chak-The ornament used for the head
weighs 44 to 88 grams.
Dora-A silver ornament of about 6 to 8
inches in length and I! in width. Composed
of two parts. Used over the fore-head a~d
fixed to the hair beneath the dhatoo by a pm.
Weighs between 88 to 132 grams.
Junti-Long attractive silver ornament
used on hair plaits. Favourite among young
women, weighs between 165 to 275 grams.
Has small silver nuts which jingle.
Linjoo-Long 'sUver stripped ornaments
used on the head. One clip is fixed to the
head and the other two ends fixed to the two
ears.
Phool-Another ornament made of thin
silver leaves pinned to the hair and spread
over the ears. This has become a jewel of the
past.
ORNAMENTS FOR TVE EAR

Kantali-Round thin silver ornament used
in upper part of the eats. Weighs between
22 to 33 grams each.
.
Mungri-This common and traditional silver or gold ornament, looks like a thin disc.
with thin circular grooves in ,it. Older women
are always seen putting it. Weighs between
22 to 33 grams.
Murki-Golden ahd most liked ornament
among young women. Slowly replacing
mungri. Weighs between 6 to 12 grams.
Kantey-A common silver or gold ornament used by women. Wives of the shopkeepers and Government officials use it.
Weighs about 15 grams.

,

Dharotu-Silver or gold ear rings. Not SO
popular. Weighs about 10 to 16 grams.
Kodolu-Small silver light ornament
weighip.g about 10 grams, used by young
women.
ORNAMENTS FOR THE NOSE

Nath-Traditional round silver or gold
ornament. Has four sharp edges. Weighs between 10 to 15 grams. Covers almost both lips
when, used.
Balo-Thin round ornament made of gold.
Coloured beads are fixed to the balo to make
it beautiful.
Footi-Most liked silver or gold ornament
among young women. It is replacing nath and
balo, shaped like a studded tili. Sometimes
used to both sides of the nose.
ORNAMENTS FOR THE NECK

Kanthi-Made of silver. Looks like a necklace and weighs between 88 to 132 grams.

Generally used by middle aged women.
Kach-Ornament made of small silver
beads, heavy and old fashioned, weighs between 165 to 200 grams.
Chander Sain-Heavy silver ornament, decorates like a necklace. Weighs 240 to 1000
grams. Other ornaments used for the decoration of the neck are dolri and tambit.
Khegwati-A thick circular silver ornament with thin ends. Worn round the neck
and is quite heavy in weight. This is now
out of date.
Jugnu-Small silver or gold ornament with
multicoloured beads studded into it. Some
are rectangulars or circular and others are
rhombus or oval in shape, fixed to jurki from
outside on the breast.
ORNAMENTS FOR THE FOREARMS

Dhagloo-Silver thick ornaments quite
heavy and weighs more than 800 grams.
Sometimes two dhagloos are joined together.
Its ends have lion shape carving.
ORNAMENTS FOR THE FINGER

Guthi-A silver or golden ring worn on
fingers and weighs about 3 grams.
Arsi-Big silver ring which sometimes has
a small mirror fixed to it.
ORNAMENTS FO,R THE WAIST

Gachhi-Only rich families can afford to
use this costly ornament. It weights about 800
grams, 5"-6" in width and worp. round the
waist.

Birth customs
Villagers pray to Jubbal-ka-devta, that a
son be born and promise to slay a goat in
devta's respect.
Precaution during pregnancy-No special
precautions are taken by the expectant
mothers during pregnancy. The women work
hard in the fields as well in the house. It is
only in the last month that the woman does
lesser work. Of course, she is not allowed to
go near cremation ground. It has been
observed that the pregnant women carry
water in tokini placed in a kilta. and bring
grass or fuel from the jungle. One elderly
member of the village revealed that in some
cases, women give birth to children in the
fields. To support he quoted an example
where the daughter-in-law of Ranvir of Chergaon gave birth to a child in a nearby field.

Diet-A pregnant women is given riCh diet,
like ghee, meat and milk.
Place of delivery-The delivery always
takes place in goshal. One corner of the
goshaL is broomed and white washed.
Delivery-The delivery of the child is
helped by the near female relatives or neighbours.
case they are not immediately
available the help of a low caste woman is
sought. She is given one rupee plus dhattoo.
If near relative helps, she is given five rupees
and a piece of dhattoo. After cleaning thl'
child and the mother, both of them are taken
to pando No time is noted by any member of
the family because people do not get the
horoscope of the child prepared.
'-,

In

Days of the birth purification-Befol'f.
bringing the mother to pand, she is given a
spoonful mixture of ghee, milk and the cow'~
urine gonch. This mixture is sprinkled in the
pand with the twigs of baikri shrub. In pand
the mother and child are kept for 15 days.
After this they are taken to the upper storey,
where she is given a spoonful of the mixture
again. This is sprinkled all over the
house. This is known shudi. If some relatives
or a pandit happens to visit the family during
days of impurity, no meals are served to him
from the family kitchen for the first three
days. The'me21s supplied to him from a kitchen in a separate house. Even the family
devta is not worshipped for the first three
days.

If a family pandit comes with green turf
known as joob in their dialect, to shower
congratulations upon the family, he is given
one rupee and some grains. In the c.ase of
first boy, if the father has taken any vow to
please his family devta, he fulfils his promise
after 15 days, whatever the cost be.
In the case of first male child in a Thakur
or Rajput family, relatives and low castes
people come with joob in their hands and
after congratulations, to the parents and
prayer for the child's long life. The proudy
father keeps all joob in his cap for a few
days. Sometimes relatives give some cash
with joob, which however is returned with
the same amount. The relatives and friends
are given a grand dinner consist;ng of meat
and rice. Kalis are given food-grains.

The diet of the mother-The diet of the
mother for a month is simple which may be
easily digestible. This consists of badi, rich
in ghee and over boiled rice. The poor families use mustard oil in place of ghee and the
sturdy women digest this. The rich families
have now st'arted giving soonth cooked in
pure desi ghee to the mother.
Kori-ki-jurki-After the birth, the child is
wrapped in a piece of cloth for two or three
days. Then he Is dressed in small home
stitched clothes, known as kori~ki-jurki. No
bad or good omen is considered if the child
cuts upper or lower teeth first. In fact the
people are free from such superstitions and
have much faith in the devta.
Name giving-A pandit is not consulted
for giving the name to the child. Usually the
child is named in the third month by some
elderly lady. It has' been observed that the
name of the child, in some cases, depends upon various factors, like the day or month when
the child was born. If a child is born on Wednesday or Thursday, he is given the name of
Budhu or Braistoo, the names derived frem
budhawar-Wednesday and braispatwarThursday. If a child is born in the Vikrami
month of Bhadra his name is given as Bhadraa. This custom is more in the low caste
families.
Boring oJ nose and ears-The custom of
bering a nose and ears of a girl is held when
she attains the age of about 3 years. The boring is done by simply pricking with a ne~dle
and a pucca thin thread is given a knot. The
place of bori!).g is first appl.ied wit!: butter so
that the child may feel little pam at the
moment of boring. The child is given some
sweets to eat so that she may facilitate the
boring work.. In the past, the boring ?f male
child's ears was also common, but thIS practice is not prevalent now.

Hair cutting-No significant event is held
in .connection with the cutting of the boy's
haIr called mundan samkar. Hair are removed w~e~ child is one or two years old. Family
pandtt I~ asked to find out an auspicious day
for cuttIng the hair. In case he is not available, the elder family member, himself performs the ceremony and sometimes takes the
help of a barber. Among Kolis, hair are re!ll0ved by any of the relatives. Hair are kept
m house. wrapped in cow-dung. On that day,
the famIly prepares good food and in some
cases wine is taken. Some families take hair
to Hatk9ti and offer to goddess Durga. They
also offer Rs. 1.25 nP. and some halwa. There
is <;me more peculiar way for disposing of the
haIr. They are taken to the high peaks of
Chansal or Kakapattam called danda by a
villager who goes with goats and sheep. At
danda .he performs a li~tle puja and place_;
the haIr on the top. For this he is given
one rupee and some grains. The child is taken
out of the house in the fifth or sixth month.
. Janeo-The custom of putting on janeo
1S not common here. The elderly people told
that no one in the village wears janeo.
. SuperstitioTIs-There are certain superstitions regarding birth customs. If a child always keeps crying or remains inactive, the
family devta is consulted. Prayers are held
so that the child may get rid of all evil spiri~s
known as dak or dani. In some cases, the
family pandit is also consulted, who gives a
chaqned thread to be tied to the neck, or
forearm or legs of the child. IIf a woman
constantly looses her child, the devta is consulted through oracle. Whatever the devta
directs, is performed by the fa~ily. Devta is
given some grains and five rupees. In some
cases the pandit charms either a mole of the
woman or some other mark on her body.
'After this the woman begins to bear children
who live.
Marriages and Death Customs
Marriage customs-Marriage ih the village
is always celebrated with joy and is a colourful occasion for young as well as old person::.
Here it is celebrated in various forms.
Early talks-The first type of marriage is
celebrated in a simple way. The initiative
taken from the boy's side. Then the boy
attains the age of ten or twelve years. his
father starts -hunting a bride for his son.
Sometimes early betrothal takes place;
Villagers do not keep any horoscope of theIr
children, therefore, the question of consulting them does not arise. The only point taken
into consideration is that the boy and t~E'
girl should not belong to different caste.
Thakur must find out a bride for his son in a
Thakur family and Rajput in a Rajput family.

23

The other points taken into consideration by
the b~y's parents before negotiation are, ttat
the g:rl should be beautiful, and free from
any ~Isease or defect. The girl's parents
~nq1:ure about the conduct of the boy, his
famIly means and his physical health. Once
the father ~nd the other family members
sel~ct th~ girl, the final selection is left tu
theIr fanuly devta. The father goes, to Jabbal
devta and invokes its oracle to take orders
from devta to the negotiation. The decisIon
vests with the devta. If it is no the second
hunt f~r the girl starts. And if it fails again
~he ~ham of such searches continue. With the
yes of the devta, betrothal takes place.
Betrothal-The betrothal in the village is
an auspiCIOUS day IS found out by the oracle of the
devta. The sotha is confirmed when a mal.e
~ember o~ ~he boy's side accompanied by a
VIllager VISItS .the house of girl and gives
Rs. 5 and few pIeces of ornaments to the girl.
The boy's party and the girl's parents sit together and the engagement is final. In return
the girl's father gives one rupee to the
~hrough the party. Once sotha is completed,
1t cannot be broken except for certain definite cause or when it becomes impossible to
fulfil such promise. The causes which justify
for. the refusal to carry out the marriage may
be due to physical or mental break down on
either of the boy's or girl's part or immorality, on the part of the girl which cannot be
tolerated by the boy. It can also be due to refusal by the boy to marry the girl when he
has fallen in love with another girl or elopes
of the girl with another boy. Both the parties
are equally responsible for the bre,k down
of the betrothal. Between betrothal and the
marriage, the two families exchange various
gifts and food, like poldoo or shakdi on fairs
and festivals.

Marrige-After Ibetrothal, marriage takes
place. Early marriages are favoured when
the girl is either equal in age or older than
the boy and able to work in the fields. On the
auspicious day fixed by the pandit, the marriage party leaves for the bride's house.
Merry making goes on in the boy's house and
a goat is slain. The marriage partly consists
of about 15-30 members. This includes distinguished relatives of the groom, some respectable'villagers and a party of musicians.
The parents do not engage their sons at far
flung places and a day's journey is preferred
by both the parties. The bridegroom does not
put on sehra nor he carries any sword or co.conut in his hand. He si~ply puts on new
clothes and carries a stick. The use of pal1ci
is prevalent among Thakurs and Rajputs.

THE PEOPLE,

Among Kolis, the bridegroom walks on ~~ot
right upto the bride's house. Some famlh~s
of middle class have used horses .or mules In
place of palkis.
On reaching the destination, the marriage
party is well received and feasted nicely.
Good arrangement for their boarding is made
at the expense of the bride's side. Mostly: all
proceeding in connection with the marrIage
is held without the ,,help of the family pandit. The bridegroom is received at the gate
by the women of the bride's house ~nd is
taken to a decorated room. The respectable
old lady of the family: conducts all proceed.jngs in connection with the marriage. She
conducts the boy to room where a diva is
kept burning on a heap of grains. ':l'he bri?~
is already seated there. After bnef "pu]a,
the old lady and the bridegroom exchange
greetings with each other and he offers one
rupee at the place of puja:. This bI1ef and
simple ceremony concludes the marriage and
the groom stays in the pride's house. The
marriage party stays for a night and is entertained twice with feasts consisting of meat,
rice, pulses, vegetabl'es and some sweet
dish. Meals are served in plates, made of
leaves. Wine is frequently used in the marriages. This is the simple side of one type .of
marriage and about 90 per cent of marrIages are celebrated in this way.
But a rich Thakur or Rajput family who
wants to celebrate the marriage according to
vedic, rites seeks the help of a family pandit,
who conducts all marriage
proceedings with the chanting of the mantras and
performing puja according to the Hindu
vedic rites. All proceedings take place in
about thr,ee hours time and are solemnised
inside a bedi. In some cases bedi is-made of
tour big banana plants considered auspicious. The share of the pandit is Rs. 11 each
trom both sides, in addition to some grains
and other small sums of dandakshana which
he receives in the course .of marriage proceedings.

a

l."t:./I..~ uay LIlt: rnarnage party returns to
their home accompanied by the bride with as
much persons from bride's side as there are
in the marriage party. This party from
bride's side stays for one night and then returns to their homes. Th~ visiting party is
entertained with rice, meat and local distilled wine.

Maklawa-Maklawa is known as danooch.
Usually it is held after one month. Before
this ceremony, the bride's father sends good
dishes consisting of paldoo and bade to his

daughter. The couple goes to in-law's house
taking one kilta of cooked 'rice balls, and
paldoo. This is known as kilti. The couple
returns after a few days stay.
The bajawalas are given a rupee and four
annas each after the ceremony is over. This
is exclusive of the meals and food which they
get at both the ends during the celebrations.
The devta is offered five rupees through his
oracle.

<

Dowry-The dowry is of. a very nominal
nature and depends upon one's economic resources. Rich families give five Or six pieces
or ornaments like pnooli, dhangloo, kanthi,
rnurki, chak. A pair of good clothes and a
Dedding is given. Utensils \are not given.
Among poor families, one ornament and a
few other things form the dowry_
Another type of marriage is unique in its
proceedings. Sotha ceremony takes place in
the same way as in the first type of marriage.
When the two parties fix a date for marriage
four or five persons from the bridegroom's
side go to the bride's house and stay there for
a night. They are-entertained with good food
and wine. Next day the parents of the Bride
send their daugter with the party to the
groom's house. About 10-15 persons of her
village accompany the bride and stay for a
night in the house of bridegroom where they
are entertained nicely. In this marriage, boy
does not go to the bride's house. This type of
marriage suits many class families as no
extravagant expenditure is incurred.
The 3rd type of marriage is where the
parents of both sides exchange their daughters for their sons. This is known as dorimitri. Devta is consulted and regular sotha
ceremony is observed. Tne marriage in this
type is held in either of the two ways as explained above. The marriages however takes
place at different dates. There is yet another
form of marriage by eloping with a girl of
another village. This is known as dhara.
Efforts from the boy's side to persuade the
girl's parent to agree to the marriage in the
regular course are ll).ade. If the girl's parents
d~cline the requests, then the marriage is
held by dhara. The run-away is decided by
the girl_gnd the boy in a fair or festival. The
boy with two or three friends comes and
elopes away the girl. 'In case some relatives
·resist at that time but the use of force from
the boy's side cannot be ruled out. The girl
is given all receptions befitting her at the
boy's house. At the.news of the elopment her
parents show anger by taking the matter to
