the Police or court. The matter cools down
after a brief pause. When both parties agree,
girl's father comes to boy's house with. some
delicious food for the couple. After thIS the
parties live on cord,ial terms. The girl's fath~r
gives some ornaments and clothes to hlS
daughter. Usually father of the boy does not
resist this type of IlJ.arriage.

Reet-The last type of marriage is known
as Teet or dheri. If a woman dislikes her husband, she is at liberty to marry another person 0:11 her choice. The second husband has to
pay some compensation. The amount is
mutually agpE;ed upon in the presence of few
relatives. This is known as Teet or dheri
Lp.na. Elderly people tell that a woman generally has a change of two husbands. No sp~cial ceremony is held in this marriage. The
woman simply enters the new house of new
husband! after reet is completed. There is one
noticeable point in this. If a woman enters
the house of second husband without the formal consent of the first husband, the second
husband shall have to pay double the amount
of reet to be mutually decided.
, A marriage of this type took place during
the investigator's stay in the village. J anki
Dass' daughter remarried for which he took
Rs. 1,000;as dheri-Iena. Out of this sum of
Rs. 960 was given to first husband and the
balance of Rs. 40 was utilized by Janki Dass
for marriage expenditure.
Widow remarriages are allowed but a
woman canr..ot marry before the period of six
months after the death of her husband.
Child marriage is not common now. The
average age of a boy and girl at marriage may
be 16 and 14 years. Some times betrothal
takes place when the boy and girl are very
sm.all. Shiv Saran's son was engaged when
he was 4 years old.
Some times. a daughter older than a son is
preferred by boy's parents, who after marrir
age helps in agricultural operations. In Chergaon there are four such cases where wives
are older than husbands.
No inter-caste marriage has come to our
notice in the village, nor is welcomed. During State's regime if a Swarn married a
Harijan girl, the matter was at once reported to the ruler. The <:Quple was asked to per-

25

form shudhi by offering some cash to the
. ruler in the shape of line or nazrana and by
slaying a goat in honour of the devta. In case
a Harijan boy marries a Swam girl, the boy
was fined by the ruler. After f"'JZrana and
fine, the couple was separated and the girl
had to live with her parents.
Polygamous and polyandrous marriages
are found. The latter is not profusely practised. Marriages are governed by economic
and hiologi'cal factors. Two or more brothers
with meagre economic resources may afford
to have one wife 'in common and a rich man
would like to have one or more wives to do
the agricultural work. Again a man having
no brothers with small income may engage
only one wife.
Here people trace poryandrous maIT13g~
\0 the times of Pandav brothers who had

Draupdi as their common wife. They believe
that this marriage strengthens the family
ties, avoids its fragmentations and family
members feel a sense of security. The family
does not increase so rapidly. A polyandrous
wife maintains the balance of unity and love
among brothers. In case of division her saying is honoured in regard to the distribution
of children among brothers.
Death rites-A piece of cloth is kept below the dying man. If a person breathes his
last on a cot, no special ritual is observed.
The dying person need not be removed to
the ground fioor. A small amount of
Rs. 1.20nP., Some kungu and kesar is put into
the mouth of the dying man. In' their dialect
this is known as shanesha. A few ganga jaZ
drops are put into the mouth. The eyes
and mouth are closed and limbs are straightened. Hands are kept on the chest. When
the person has breathed his last the body is
covered with white cloth known as jafJTi.
The body of the deceased is not washed. The
head is kept towards any direction. Baja"-Dalas are called who used to play ulta-baja
by beating the drums in sad tunes.

About two decades ago, the dead bodies
in Thakur and Rajput families were kept
for a day or two so that they could be
mourned by relatives who could come from
distant places. The relatives are sent for by
deputing Kolis to convey the condolence
message to relatives of the deceased. Sometimes two or three hundred relatives and
others gather. When the father of Thakur
Lila Singh expired, more than seven: hundred women gathered and expressed their
condolence.

26

THE PEOPLE,

\The presence of maha bhahman or Acharaj is not necessary to perform last rites ..
Death is followed by cremation. A bier is
made by the village carpenter. This is known
as khat. Wooden handles known as hasti
are fixed to khat. The Badhi is given a rupee
for making this. Two, four or six of the nearest relatives carry the dead body to the
burning place known as marvi where the
pyre is built in a rectangular shape. The pyre
is known as jhoher and the place where the
pyre is made, is known as malthan. After the
pyre is ready, the dead body is placed ,over
it. All the extra clothes covering the body
except the coffin in which the body is wrap-,
ped are removed. The sons remove their
hair and one of the bajawala does this. No
pinds are offeredi to th'e deoeased. After
completing the simple rite, the pyre is lit by
the chief mourner. Pure desi ghee is sprinkled over the pyre so that it is ablaze and the
entire body is consumed by the flames.
After a while those who accompany the
dead body, return to their homes except the
chief mourner and other 4-6 persons.
These persons bathe in the stream before
they return home. When the chief mourner
sees that the body is consumed by the
flames, he touches the deceased's skull with
a long pole. The rite is called kepal kriya or
chopna in their dialect. Then he returns
hoine where he is again consoled by the
gathering. The nearest relatives stay for five
to seven days. H death takes place before
noon, the dah sanskar is performed the same
day and if it takes place in the evening or at
night, the cremation is put off till next
mornjng. AJ watch is kept over the whole
night lest the dead body wakes up and
frightens every one.
As a rule no food is cooked in the house
where death has taken place. The food for
the family and others are cooked in a separate house. The charaj -is sent for after three
days of the death. He purifies the family by
a little puja on the bank of the river. Then
pinds are offered to the deceased soul. These
are made of rice. All cooking on that day is
held on the bank of the river where food is
served. After this ceremony, the family
starts taking fried meals. The clothes of the
deceased are given partly to charaj and
partly to the nath who claims a share.
Next day, the ashes are washed away#into
the stream. Before doing it one of the turi is
allowed to search out Re. 1 and annas four
which were placed in deceased's mouth. In
case of some families, some bones are tied in
a new piece of cloth and kept to be sent to
liardwar for immersion in the Ganges,

THEIR

CULTURg AND

MATERIAL EQUIPMENT

The days of impurity coincide with the
day of kriya and it differs from caste. Among
Rajputs and Thakurs the final purification
kriya is held on the eleventh day. Among
Kolis, the day of purification takes place on
the 7th day. The family pandit does not take
his meals till a goat is slain. Yearly feedings
known as shradhs are also held. It is prohibited to take, meat on these days. Milk, curd,
vegetables, pulses are served.
Children below 9 months are buried,
others are burnt quite near the vmage on
the bank of the stream. Thakurs and Rajputs and Kolis use their own separate
marvis.
If a cat crosses the dead body superstition
goes that the dead person gets up and makes
dreadful sounds and frightens the people. It
is, therefore, considered necessary that a
watchful eye over the dead body is kept till
cremation. This is a superstition. Some keep
cloth and plenty of wood well stocked in the
house for an emergency in: case anyone dies.

III. Economy
Rural economy depends upon many aspects like agriculture and significant subsidiary occupations. The integration of all such
aspects revolves round the economic wheel.
Besides these, natural gifts like forests, rearing up of cattle, help to get a little more
amount. Village crafts and industries cannot
be dropped out. These are important aspects
of rural economy as agriculture is. A portion
of village population depends wholly or
partly on such occupation.

Agriculture is mainly the task of Thakurs
and Rajputs. A large number of rural crafts
remain the monopoly of lower castes. In
Chergaon, carpentry and working on stones
is the monopoly of Badhi. Lahar is mainly
supposed to make iron & agricultural implements. Kolis also dispose of dead cattle and
do leather work.

take up the classification of land for the' last
eleven years in Table No. -h.

Crops-The seasonal cycle often allows
two harvests in a year commonly known
kharif and rabi. The crops sown with the
beginning of monsoon in late .J:une and harvested' towards the end of October are
known as Kharif. These crops require more
water. The second crop season begins in early
November and the crops are harvested in
April and May are known as rabi.
Jinswar statements for khanf and rabi for
the last ten years have been taken from Lal
Kitab of the village Patwari given in Tables
Nos. 19-20. The village has perennial kuhI
irrigation. It is the khanf cropping, where
nahari sowing occupies a predominavt place.
The principle crops sown during khan! are:
paddy: chena, koda and chilai. The larg~st
area is occupied by paddy. It may be stated
here that whole of the Rohru Tehsil is called the granary of rice. Taking the figures
of 1961. 64 acres out of 183 acres, was occupied: by paddy, thus giving the percentage
of 34.8 over the total annual khanf cropping
area.
The crops occupying the largest areas in
vahi harvests are wheat and barley. During
1961, wheat and barley were sown in 34.1.
and 57.7 per cent of the total sown area. As
regards distribution of the sown areas between kharif and rabi crops, there is not
marked change except that the area being
put"'under paddy during kharif, and wheat
and barley during rabi is on a slight increase.
The crops are sown in a rotation method in
the following manner:Kharif

Seeds-The farmers bother little about the
benefits of improved: seeds for different
crops. They give preference to their own
seeds kept aside from the previous harvest.
The gram sewak and agriculture inspectl)r,
recently, have been persuading farmers
to us~ improved seeds given at cheap rates.
The unportant seeds are V.S. hybrid-maize,
~-21 _wheat and upto-date potatoes. Sometimes Improved seeds are given on an exchange ba.sis. The- quantity of seeds given to
a farmer lS taken back after the farmer has
harvested the improved crops. Vegetable
seeds a~e procured_by the shopkeepers who
have kItchen gardens behind their houses.
Irrigation-In all, there are five irrigation
channels, known as kuhls. They water the
fields. These have been drawn from Andhra
stream in Jugaha Valley. Three kuhls flow
to the left side of the Andhra stream covering all fields. around the main village. Two
kuhls feed the fields in Bhonda area opposite
the main village. The four kuhls are Devsari, Akhali, Chadyara and Badinahar. The
fifth kuhl on the right of the stream has nof
been named as yet. These kuhls have been
construct~d ,:>y t~e villagers many a decade
back. Ir~IgatlOn IS received for both khari!
a?d rabt harvests. The internal distribution
of water between the cultivators is fair and
free by many small kuhls made on the terraces o~ ~he fie.lds. Whenever any kuh I
breaks, It IS repaired by the villagers.
DU.ring the investigator's stay in the
khan} season, he did not observe any improved method of cultivation. The farmers
f;-lt that improved methods require more
time and labour, which they cannot afford
to do.
The Gram-s.ewak organises the laying
down of a~ncu1ture demonstration plots
before the VIllagers by taking a small piece
of land. He works on that plot throughout
the harvest .~th his new methods, improved
seeds, fertlhz~rs and agricultural implements and .compares ~is produce with those
of the cultlvators, whlCh is more.
Manur~-The local manure is the mixfure
of dro~pmgs and the dry needles of cheel
and katZ !rees. The farmers feel that their
manure lS more fertile and better than

chemical fertilizers. They' spread dry neeaies
and IJther gre€n leaves in goshal beneath the
cattle every day and remove these after
about a week. Then it is taken to al secure
place before the house or in a corner of a
field from where it; can easily be transferred
to the fields. The manure is used when it is
decomposed after keeping for a long time
in the sun. The storing of the manure in
front pf the house is not hygienic. With tlie
help of some farmers, community development staff dug compost pits where the farmers are advised to store their home-made
manure. The farmers used the pits for one
year and gave up their use next year with
the result that the pits get filled with the
mud during rainy season. In case these are
always used, best type of local manure can
be obtained as the cowdung will have sufficient time fpr decomposition in a closed pit.
The villagers are not keen towards the
use of fertilizers. There is fertiliser depot
with Lala Gauri Nand. The results were not
encouraging, less than a ton of chemical fertilizer was sold during three years. The shopkeeper offered very easy terms like long
term cre,dit. There is a belief among the
farmers that the successive use of fertilizer,
burns up the soil fertility and turns the
fields barren. The result for the first year
they say may be gopd:. lnder Dass a Raj put
farmer, . informed that on a certain plot,
during 1960, he used improved maize seeds

and applied chemical fertilizer to obtain encouraging results. But he was not successful
as the produce was an average yield. He felt
that if a crow spoiled one maize stalk, the
whole plant started rotting, whereas it does
not happen if local seeds and manure are
used. One more resiaent, Kahan Chand felt
that about three years ago he used fertIlizers
and improved maize and wheat seeds on his
holdings but the results were not encouraging. The Agriculture Inspector felt that the
farmers have not been adcpting the prescribed ways and methods for use, and hence
the results were not good. According to
them the soil of Chergaon fields have not
been chemically analysea as no one had approached with such request.
The fertilizers are npt so popular in
Chuhara illaqua as compared with other adjoining parts, like Jubbal and Kotkhai region
where hundreds of maunds of fertilizer is
used we gather.

The farmers guard their crops against
bears by keeping night watch. They also
produce sound by striking empty tins during
night over erected ma.chans. The monkeys
and crows are kept at a distance either by
raising noise or by hurling stones at them.

calendar at Table No. 23 gives the cycle of
rabi and khaTif crpppings and horticultural

village.
The agricultural practice in the hills is
rough due to the dlfficult terrain. With the
advent of sowing season, farmers are seen
carrying their wooden ploughs and driving
their bullocks to the "field'S. Manure from
cow-dung storage is carried fo the field!1 in

kiltas by women and is evenly spread over
the fields. Sowing is done by broadcast
method. Grass and other weeds growing in
the fields are removed by women with the
kuti. Paddy plantation' is carried in groups
by the women. Crops are collected: in the
individual khalyan where other operations
like threshing and winIlPwing are done. The
first step for paddy and wheat threshing is
with ~ulIocks. They tread over the spread
stalks m khalyan. Constant beating with the
long sticks is also done of crops. Winnowing
is done with shoopa.

It is observed that when the paddy is harvested it is spread in fields in symmetrical
straight layers. A bunch of paddy crops tied
in a bundle is kept standing in the middle.
The layers of paddy crops are known as
mandla and the bunch of paddy kept in the
name of family devta is known as jak. Their
custom differs with those of the farmers of
Jubbal side, where the spreading of paddy
crop is done in a different way. In Dochi village near Jubbal, it was observed that the
systematical spreading of paddy is done with
ears together of the two rows. The spreading
is known mandlar and the bunch of paddy
crops kept in the centre is known as rana.

known in their dialect are given as under:English term

Slightly more detailed operations of spme
crops are as:

39

ECONOMY
RAE! CROPS

Wheat-This is sown in September-October in kayar or bakhal land. Its chief
operations are watering, two ploughings,
ploughing and sowing. The crop is manured
when it is 3" high and harvested in MayJune. Bundles of crops are carried to houses
and kept for one-two months in tong. These
are gradually threshed, winnowed: and
grains removed.
Barley-Grown in kayar or bakhal land.
Its operations are watering, one ploughing
and levelling. Wide lines with the help of
plough are ~ade for sowing in October and
November, followed by levelling, the crop is
manured when it is 2-3"high and harvesting
in June. Crops are spread over roofs for
about a 'fortnight and then threshed with the
help of bullocks. Winnowed and grains
stored.
M asoor-Grown in bakhal land. Ploughing, levelling, sowing in 'October; manuring
in Nov.-Dec., and harvested in May.
KHARIF CROPS

Paddy-This crop is sown in kayar land
and requires water throughout its operations. Ffrst of all fields are manured and
watered. After about six ploughings in
March, these are levelled. Paddy (Nursery)
is sown by broadcast method in March-April, again fields are ploughed and planked.
Hoeing when crop is six inches high.
Now paddy seedlings are to be transplanted to other fields which are prepared as;
manuring, watering, ploughing, three levellings in knee-deep water, plantation in June,
followed by watering, hoeing at regular intervals and harvesting in October. The reaped crop is exposed to sun, for about a week
and then carried to khalyan in bundles
known as poolay. These are most attractively arranged in the fields and in the centre
they arrange a bunchplooking like a devta.
Some farmers make kha.lyan in fields by
plastering with cow-dung. The threshing is
done with the help of bullocks and husking
is carried out gradually by women throughout the winter in the okhal.

Maize-This crop is grown in bakhal
land and sown in May-June. Its operations
~re; two-three ploughings, manuring, ploughmg, lev~lli?g, sowi?g, levelling and hoeing
when SIX mches. hIgh. Again hoeing when
two feet high. It is harvested in September
and cobs are spread on the roof for a fortni~ht. ~rains are removed either by beating
wIth sticks or by hands, and grains are
stored.

Mash and KultM-This is sown on bakhal land in July and reaped in October.
One ploughing, sowing, levelling, hoeing and
reaping. It is kept for drying for a fortnight
over roof and then threshed with the help
at bullocks or sticks and winnowed.
Koda-Sown in April in bakhal land and
harve~ted: in Oct.ober. Ploughing, sowing,
levellmg, manurmg, hoeing done two
times and harvesting, Crop is kept for about
a month in khalyan for drying and then
threshed with the help of sticks. After winnowing it is sieved and grains collected.
Chena and Kangni-Sown in bakhal land
in May and June. Ploughing, sowing, levelling, hoeing and harvesting in OcWber.
Chena is threshed with feet and sieved.
Taking out some grain for seed, the rest is
parched, dried and winnowed. At the end
husking is carried out in leisure· time. Kang~
ni grain is not parched.
BathUr--Sowtrl in bakhaV or karali land
Its operations are same as that of Kangnf
but harvested a little earlier.

KachaLu-Sown in bakhal land in AprIl
and May and highly manured. It is harvest·
ed in October.
Potatoes-Sown in April-May in bakhal
land. Two ploughings, manuring, sowing,
hoeing and harvesting in October-November.
Collected and sold to traders in the fields
after keeping the required: quantity for seeds
and family' consumption. Seeds are stored
in pits.

sively grown for kitchen are, pumpkins,

Horticulture
The-re is a wide scope for the horticulture
and the farmers are being attracted towards
this. The reason is .that a small apple orchard around the forest rest·house when in
full bloom, has tempted the people. Some
apple orchards are
springing
around
Rohru. Government is advancing horticuI ture loan, refundable after five years in
easy instalments. 'The climate and othe·r
soil conditions of this place are SUitable for
horticulture. The best orchai'd which can
flourish here is of apples.
The fruit plants are collected from Government nurseries at Kotkhai, Khadrala or
Mashobra. Spmetimes, the plants are also
procured from private nurseries of Kotgarh.
Some families have taken horticulture loan
and more villagers are taking. Farmers are
setting aside a part of their holdings for
planting fruit trees. People complain that
cattle destroy their plants in the early
stages. Three families of Thakurs and Rajputs have 214 apple plants, which have survived.

Soil Conservation
The soil is rocky. There is little' soil con·
servation. Causes of erosion are the destruc·
tion of forests and excessive and uncontrolled use of grazing ~astures alon~ the bank~,

11

I)

...

The Government is taking measure to check
further big erosion. By the river sides, the
cattle are npt permitted to graze. The two
nurseries established at Seema and Cher·
gaon, would provide different species of trees
for plantation on the banks of the streams.

41

ECONOMY

The popular variety which grows tremendously quick is willow and its plantation is
favoured.
Land Refonns

Hold:ings in Chergaon are of long narrow
strips on the slope of the' hills. Not only the
holdings are small in size, but tl_:tese al'e not
situated in one compact block and are scattered in small plots.
The people are partially in favour of some
reforms. The tenants express that they may
be declared as owners of the holdings. By
this tenants would be relieved of the heavy
lab~ur they' have been putting in. Consolidation of holdings is favoure~ by th~ fanner~.
They state that with compact holdmgs, agncultural labour and ti'me would be saved and
boundary dispute between the farmers will
end. A number of petty boundary disputes
are reported to th~ Panchayat. A few rich
Thakur families having enough holdings
scattered at different places, fe'ar that in the
course of this reform, government may deprive them of the extra bulk of holdings and
distribute among the landless classes.
Price 01 Land
The Statement No. 25 classifies the sales
of land in the village during the last ten
years. The average price of .the land in Panch
Sala period from 1952-53 to 1956·57, is
Rs. 603.50 per bigha for irrigated land
and Rs. 352.05 per bigha for barani land.
During the period 1957-58 to 1961-62 the
average price of land for irrigated and barani
has been Rs. 12.05 and 53.06, respectively.
During 1956-57 two mutations took place.
The land was less than half bigha in each
case. This has been omitted and price calculated. The area sold in the period: 1952~53

to 1956-57 was more than an acre with 10
cases. In the 2nd period, _the area was 41
acres with 45 caSes of mutation. There is
vast difference in the price of land between
the two periods. Reason for difference in
the price could not -be ascertained, therefore it is difficult to assign any definite
cause for it, Informal enquiries showed that
in the past, there was a usual practice among
the zamindars to show high price to tne
revenue authorities, whereas the deed was
actually settled at a low price. For example
if a particular piece of land: was sold at
Rs. 100, the seller and buyer will pose that
the deed has been settled at Rs. 500, and
accordingly the entries were made in the
Revenue Records.

Nautor
Since 1960 to date, fifteen nautor land
cases were granted. The names along with
caste of the persons, who have acquired land
during the last eleven years are given in
table 26. In seven cases 6 persons belonging to scheduled-caste" have acquired the
land. 8 cases of nautors stand in the names
of Rcijputs and Thakurs. Little mountaineous area or some slopy fragments lying
within the village area have been purchased.
Before 1960, the Government rates for acquiring new land was Rs. 5 and Rs. 25 per
bigha for the land lying outside and within the village boundary. But now the rates
have been doubled to Rs. 10 and Rs. 50 per
bigha_ Except for the case dated 25-11-1957,
the price of the land: have been calculated
at the above rates. The case dated 25-11-57
is a penalty case as told by the village Patwad. The peasant first illegally occup~ed the
area and then filed an application for nautor.
The penalty imposed was Rs. 40 plus Rs. 51
being the price of the land.

Mortgage
A farmer is often in need of money
'Nhich is borrowed from rich zantinda·rs. He
mortgages his possession or property as security which is recovered at payment of debt
at agreed time. In Chergaon, poor peasants
go to the money lenders and mortgage their
property. The custom of mortgaging j~welleries is also common. The money paId to
the peasant by the landlord is approximately half the value of jewellery. In addition, the mortgagee charges interest at the
rate of 25 per cent on the mortgage debt.

If the poor peasant is not in a position to

repay the debt, the ornaments become the
property of mortgagee. Thakurs and Rajputs
are the principal mortgagees for Kolis. A
few families of Thakurs and Rajputs also had
to mortgage their jewellery with rich Thakur and Rajput families. The mortgagee ties
a small chit to the different ornaments for
distinguishing the owners. Now this custom
is fading away. With the availability of employment pn roads and building constructions, the peasants are able to meet their
urgent and daily requirements by earning
cash wages. Sometimes, the utensils are also

ECONOMY

Many provisions, desired by both the parties, are entered in the Revenue Records
with the mortgage deed. These are known as
chagota and nagadi lagan. Chagota deed
takes pJace when mortgagee is unable to
cultivate the land. Under it, the mortgaged
land remains under the plough of the mortgager and some grains are given to the
mortgagee at two harvests. The quantity of
chagota is fixed by both the parties. Under
nagadi lagan, the mortgagor pays some cash
annmdly to the martgagee. Usually it is
equal to the amount of interest calculated
at the mortgaged debt. There is one more
clause, that a mortgagee can sell the deed
to an<?ther zamindar along with the rights as
explamed above under chagota or nagadi

mortgaged to meet the urgent necessities of
life.
Agricultural lands are also mortgaged by
the needy peasants when they need money
for marriage or to construct a new house or
to repay old debt. Since 1952-53, 19 cases of
mortgages were registered with the. Revenue Department' with highest number of
cases during 1961-62, table No. 27.
The price of the mortgaged land does not
indica_te the actual market price. A needy
peasant may mortgage his land at a low
price. A zamindar cares little for the market
price. On the other hand, a peasant is compelled to mortgage his land at low price
under pressure of d:ebt of his sahukar, and
he does not hesitate to bow before the wishes
of his sahukar.

Kedemption

Cases of redeeming the mortgaged land
also take place. Sometimes a part of the'
land is redeemed instead of the whole mortgaged land. Some of the cases of redemption
are effected by the sale of a part or whole
of the land. Table No. 28 shows the number
of redemption cases that took place between
the years 1952-53 to 1961-62.
Tenancy System

In many cases the owner of a small area
of land is also the' tenant of another plot.
There are f#Jur such families consisting of
seventeen persons. 28 persons residing in 11
households are tenants. Practically, all tenants do not enjoy the right of occupancy. The
owners frequently make fear the tenants by
drawing their attention to ejection, when the
latter neglect their duties towards them.
The common term used here for a t-enant is

types of tenancy are

Rigad-Under this system, a man borrows
money from a rich family. When the former is not in a position to return the debt
the rich family asks his debtor to do 8.gricul~
tural labour On his fields ..Grains are given
at two harvests to the· debtor or his family
members whosoever works. The rates of
such wages in kind are; 6 bhars of grain for
a man and 3 bhars for a woman. On the
other hand, the amount of debt which
has increased with the addition of' interest
still stands. In this way rigad tenancy passe~
on from father to his son. This type of
tenancy still exists in the village.
. Basin-Under this system, a rich farmer
IBves a pier€' of agricultural holding and a
small housp. to another poor family. In return he dp.mands agricultural work to be
done in his own agricultural fields. Besides,

all outside work like carrying of some household: goods from one place to another are

the village.
Buithiyarey-This system of terian~j" does.
not exist in the village now, though it was
common during the pre-infil,ependence' era.
A man used to take vast agricultural holdings belonging to Raja o.n batai system, further distributed the same agricultural holdings among some families who cultivated the
land. The cultivators of these agricultural
holdings were called baithiyare'y. or baithoo.
Harijans are mostly tenants. They have
been often expressing their reseiibpent
against the prevalence of this age-old tenancy
system. They cannot afford to keep good animals and live leisurely.
Animal Husbandry
Cattle are an indispensable asset to village life <:pmposed of agricultural activities.

Agriculture in Chergaon is followed on the
old pattern, cattle are also old, small,
rickety and a great liability often. Here,
farmers have no liking for artificial fertilizers, and rely upon their traditional home
made manure. Hides and o.'ry cow dung are
the other useful products of cattle wealth.
Cattle of several types are reared up. Sliebuffaloes are reared by rich families of
Thakurs and Rajputs. The breeds of t1ie
cattle are poor. They could improve if there
were bulls of an improved variety. A red
Sindhi bull is kept at the Civil Veterinary
Hospital, Rohru, but the 9 miles distance
makes it not always easy for farmers tn
take their cows to Rohru. A cow gives about
Ii seers of milk per day and a she-buffaloe
gives 5 seers per day. 16 households have kept large herds of- goats and sheep immensefully useful for wool. Sheep and goats
·are sheered twice a year. About half seer
of wool is obtained at each sheering. First
sheering is held '. before October and the
second in the month of April.

Blacksmithy-There is another family of
Lohar in the village who attends to useful
work of mending old iron agricultural tools
and making new ones. He wholly depends
upon this industry. For six months in a
year, he works in another village because
he does not find enough work tp make both
ends meet. He also makes two useful articles which display hIS craft. They are local
locks and iron angithis. Every villager has
at least one big lock for his kuthar. For better and bigger locks and angithis the farmers
place their demands with skilled craftsmen
living in Tikkri and Pekha Devadi. A big
lock in Thakur Lila Singh's Kuthar weighs
more than 2 seers. Its one foot long key
bear's the ma~ufacturihg date inscribed in
Hindi as, 2010 Samat. 25 Asauj, .
These two articles are heavy and voluminous in size. Angithis weigh 10 to 20 seers
and are rectangular, or hexagon or octagon
in shape. The artisan takes long time in preparing these articles, therefore the wages for
manufacturing, increase the total cost of
production. Now, villagers do not go in for
such costly items. To meet their present need
they purchase cheap machine made locks and
angithis. The locally made angithi is decorated with flowery brass plates with brass
stands, the upper ends of which are made
like Lion faces-shermukh. On four corners
heavy chains are fixed in the ring holes pre~
pared for the purpose. It would not be out

to practise this craft in the village by installing the automatic 100m procured by him
from the Block Dev~lopment authorities at
half subsidy price, costing Rs. 120.
Shoe-making'-The making of traditional
types of shoes, known as lawatey is practically vanishing. This craft was being practised by the Koli community of the village.
Kalis, if asked by rich and influential Thakur
and Rajput families, still prepare such shoes.
Reason for the gradual decline of thiS" craft

49
ECONOMY

is due to the liking for cheap machine made
shoes. The second reason is that the Kalis
have stopped the proo:uction of this craft
due to certain social factors. One cobbler
from Ranika Raipur in Punjab has settled
near the village and meets the demands in
respect of cheap hand made shoes.
Whenever a cattle dies, it is handed over
to the Kolis, who remove it away to the bank
of the stream or river. The owner of the
cattle is given one pair of lawatev and one'
piece of par,j_ya, an agricultural i~ple~ent
free and the rest of the carcase IS utlhzed
by the Koli.
The artisan works on this craft. in spare
time in a corner of his rpom. He owns few
tools procured locallyRampa-For cutting leather.
Sua-A leather borer.
Zamboor-For putting leather cord.
Quai-Like anvil used in making shoes.
Sui-For preparing upper portion of

lawatey.

Luwatey are very warm during wint~r
and consist of two parts. The lower part. IS
made of two or three layers of leather whIle
the upper' portion consists of .knitted cloth
of multi-coloured threads WhICh resembles
with the upper portion of poo~a. The .lJJwer
portion is made by the artIsan hImself
whereas the upper portion is knitted by the
artisan's wife. Sometimes, the customer also
helps in knitting the upper portion. The
coloured threads are purchased from local
banias. The approximate cost of a pair of
lawatey as revealed by the artisan comes out

Spinning--Some spinning is done during
winter. The nights are long and the household members sit in pand around the chool
and spin wool. Tools for spinning are takdi
operated by twisting fingers. Another tool
is known as koru operated by plaCing on

thigh or in between two palms. Koru is
bigger than takdi. During night all members
operate tukdi and during day time, men
while moving in the village go on spinning
on koru. The local wool of their sheep is not
sufficient to meet their requirement, therefore, in the past, people used to go to Rampur to purchase wool during Lavi fair. But
now-a-days, they prefer to buy wool from
Dodra Kawar illaqa. The villagers feel that
the wool of the Dodra-Kawar is inferior to
that of Rampur. Jurkhi made out of their
hand spun wool lasts upto three or four
years if worn throughout the year. The
villagers also spin goat's hair from which
kharcha or fakula is made. I.t is used as a
rug.
~ee keeping is practised by about one
thIrd of the houses who hcwe a hive or two.
This is a paying industry and adds a little
to the econ?my of the people. In goshaZ,
small bee hIve cells are mad:e in the wall
of the house where bees are popularised.
When the hives are full of honey a man
wrapped .in black clothes collects the honey
~urmg Illght. In one hand he carries burnmg dry cowdung cake or burning cotton
clothes and with the second hand the hives
~re removed into a prato The honey collected
IS l?artly used by the house members with
rotts as. a. substitute for vegetables, and the
rest of It IS sold to local bunias and Government Officials @ Rs. 2.50 P. per seer. The
V:'ax tha.t is collected is used for medicines
lIke CUrIng eye sore. Sometimes it is given
to village Kolis who make shoes.
V:illagers also extract oil from the wild
area is abundant
m chuh trees. In its peak season during
summer, m~n and women collect chuH in
large quantIty. These are kept in sun for
two-three days to dry. The hard nuts are
broken. and _kernels ~re dried on a fire pan
for, a ~Ittle tIme and then ground. The oil is
extracted by squeezing in between the
hands. The :remainder is given to cattle which
serves as rIch f~daer. The villagers feel, oil
made. at home IS mO.re tasty and nourishing
than the mus.tat:d 011 sold in the market.
Food cooked III this oil is very delicious.

There is a Brahman owning a tiny eat~ng
house. He has migrated from Kangra. He

51

ECONOMY

prepares meals at the order of the travellers.
On his meagre income, he has a big family
to support. Another hotelwala is running his
hotel in the Bhonda area. He is a local man
of Chelala village and his hotel is known by
the name of Chuharoo hotel. There are no
monthly customers at these hotels.
A mochi has come'from Rani-ka-Raipur. He
makes and sells tanned leather. Being new
he refrains from credit. This mochi lives in
a rented house in Bhonda and undertakes repair of old shoes for which his charges are
nominal.
A roving barber cuts _the hair of people
here and of'surrounding areas. For four days
hi' a week, he stays in the village and for
other three days, he visits the surrounding
villages in a range of 14 miles. While in
Chergaon, the four shop-keepers of Kangra
have agreed to stay for one day 'each with
them where he is served food and tea. He
renders free ~ervice to all the members of
their household including the servants. His
usual charges from villagers for hair cut and
shave are four annas and two annas. On occasions like marriages or births in a bania
family, he acts a's a massenger and attends
to other work. He is seen trimming the pails.

right of cutting of hay against payment of
nominal revenue. In addition vast area of
other waste land also meets to. the requirement of green grass as well as hay.
We may make a passing mention of colh~cting hay for winter months. From the
early Bhadra women start collecting green
grass from the jungles, bring it to village
and spread it in the sun. In a single sunny
day green grass gets dry. It is collected with
wooden fork known as augshu and long thick
ropes are made, known as' bali. Men help in
drying and making of balies. The grass is cut
and carried to the village by the women.
They go to jungle twice to fetch grass. Once
before noon after taking morning meals and
the second time in the afternoon. In some
cases, women make hay in the jungle, and
bring balies home. The ,balies are kept in the
verandah. Sometimes they are hung from
the tree branches.
.
Poor families of Kolis sell dry grass to
rich families of Thakurs and Rajputs. They
also sell green grass to local banias at the
rate'of one pulla-a bundle of "grass, for one
anna. Dry grass is sold @ 0.25 Paise per
bali. Banias also make hay, but their collection is insufficient to meet their demand.
They have kept mules besides cattle.

Two village tailors are Kolis. They do agriculture work besides stitching clothes. A
new tailor from Kangra District has installed his shop recently. A Koli, named Tawarsi
has been given a free sewing machine .by the
Block Development Officer. He asked for this.
The case was recommended by the village
Panchayat. The villagers get their clothes
stitched by the old tailors as the new tailor
from Kangra is not able to stitch the clothes
of the village fashion. However he meets the
requirement of Government officials and
local banias. The rates .£or stitching the different clothes are:Name Of Clothes stitched

Pastures and Fire-woad-People have vast

Fishing
Fishing without a licence is prohibited in
Andhra and Pabber. In Chergaon about
ninety per cent of the population "is nonvegetarian, the two streams meet their supply of fish, The villagers do not say openly
that they catch fish. But it was known that
about a dozen fishing nets are kept by them.
People catch fish either under darkness
of night or in the early hours of morning. Fish are always plentiful in the stream,
but the best time for catching them is during
rainy season when due to the gushing water,
fish take shelter under big boulders on the
sides of banks where the flow of water is
not heavy.

Rural Indebtedness

Farmers with limited resources always
need money. Under the stress of circumstan~
ces, they have to borrow to finance agricul~
tural operations and to tide over the bad
years.
Out of 88 households, 41 families claim to

be under debt. The total debt is nearly
Rs. 37,090. The table Nos. 37 and 38 show the
causes for the debt. Rs. 5,500 is the total
amount of Taccavi loan taken by the villa~
gers. There was a liti'gation between a Koli
and bania over one, house belonging to the
former and illegally occupied by the bania.
A great amount of money was spent by both
the parties. The Koli had to go to money lenders to borrow money. In the law court,
bania lost and had to pay Rs. 4,000 to the
Koli, who di<i not repay the debt and spent
the amount on other needs. A Rajput family
has run into heavy debt on account of giving
wine on credit baSis. He complained that
people bought wil!~ from his shop and did

not pay. He ran Into debt to the amount of
Rs. 4,000. One famtly of Badhi ran into heavy
debt during his daughter's marriage. It was
rumoured that he is selling his house just to
clear the debt. Some families spend 'reckless~
lyon social ceremonies like marriages, festi~
vals and so on. One family is under debt on
account of sickness. The other general causes
of indebtedness in the village are house cohs~
tructlon and to meet ordinary wants.
'

Rich Thakurs and Rajputs are the money
lenders in the village. There is one bania,
who also deals in money-lending. In the past,
farmer used to go to the money-lender to
borrow money, who used to give him the
amount after getting a deed written in tankri script. Before giving the amount the
money-lender used to deduct 'some amount
under the custom gant kholna. The rate of
such custom was annas -!2!~ pet; l!upee. :8"ythis ~U&tQm it waS meant that a relaticmship
between a money-lender and his debtor has
begun which perhaps will never end.

EcbNOMY

The interest charged was four annas per
rupee armually. Th~s was collected at the
end of each year. A farmer was hardly able
to pay the loan leave aside the interest.
Whenever the money-lender visit~d the
house of his debtor he was treated well, respected and entertained to wine and meat.
After a stay of a day or two and collecting
a bit of the interest, the money-lender and
his party used to proceed to another debtor.
The money-lender used to collect huge sum
by way of collecting interest and his principal remained the same. And the d~bt was inherited by the sons on the death of their
fathers.
The relationship between a money-lender
and his debtor, in the course of time created
the tenancy system. Instead of paying interest, tlie tenants-rigad are now doing
agricultural labour to escape the fury of
zamindars.

Coinage, Weights and Measures
People are familiar with new coinage with
their constant dealing with the shop-keepers.
In the past, they used to weigh articles in
their traditional weighing scale known ;18
tool. This had a rod to one end of which is
tied scale made of cotton. The articles for
weighing are put in the scale and are balanced with a weight hung on the other end. The
weig.q.t is adjusted according to the quantity
to weight. The common traditional weights
are:-

18 sarsai
1 kacha seer
5 kacha seers
1 patha
16 pathas
1 bhar
20 bhars
1 joan or 1 khar
20 khars
1 kharsha
A patha is a measuring utensil which contains' 11- seer of rice. There is another small
patha w,hich contains half the quantity of
the big patha.
.
The traditional way of measuring the
length of a cloth still exists among the villagers. They are:1 mushtown

156 from elbow to fist.

1 hath

IS' from elbow to tip of middle
finger.

1 baith

6' from tip·of thumb to tip of small
finger.

1 kudaith

5' from tip of thumb to tip of first
finger.

1 footoo

12' two fists together with thumbs,
stretched together.

With shopkeepers, they buy the cloth in
terms of yards which later on is explained
in terms of metres ngw!

Pediars and Casuai Visitors
Many from different parts visit Chergaon
and sell little articles against cash payment.
They also exchange their articles for the
grains. Some people yho come are:1. An old Sikh from Amritsar side visits
the village twice in a year and collects counterfeit coins for which he gives half the value
of such coins. In the village, he is known as
khotey paisay waIa, and is very popular
among villagers.

2. A party of two-three men from J agadhri side also visits the village and purchase
old and. unserviceable utensils against half
the cost paid in cash. The party visits once
a year.
3. A few Tibetans from Kinnaur side visit
the village during winter to sell their petty
things like heeng, jeera, zayfal, sheelajeet
and beads. They get the price either in cash
or in kir..d. A few Kinnauras from Kinnaur
District also come to sell their goats and
sheep. These people enter Rohru valley via
Sangla-Buranghati route.

4. Some from Sirmur District visit this
illaqa to sell their ginger against cash or
kind.
5. A party of potters from Mandi and Bilaspur Districts come to this illaqa. They make
the earthen pots on the spot and sell them
against cash or kind.

6. Some fortune tellers-Pandits from
Garhwal also pay frequent visits and collect
cereals.'

Other people who pass through the village
or some-times stay for a night in the village
are, Gujjars who go the high peaks in Chanshal, N epalis and people from Mandi and
Bilaspur Districts who are engaged as labourers by the Forest contractors, age.r.,ts of big
firms who visit the shopkeepers, and people
from Dodrakawar; who take their sheep
and goats to pastures 'in Bilaspur, Dhami,
Arki, Tatapani, Mahlog and Solon during
winter.
.

monthly income is above Rs. 101. He is to
support a large family.
Two Sud shopkeepers are very rich and
their monthly income is above Rs. 101 each.
Out of the two Lohar households, one
works on iron and another on silv;ersmithy,
besides doing agriculture. Their monthly income is between Rs. 76-100 and.Rs. 26-50.

These statements show that the main expenditure . for all castes are food, clothing
and smo~mg. Only a few artisan communities purchase little quantity of food grains
from local shopkeepers and others manage
to meet fopd reqUIrement from their lana
produce. Agricultural labourers however are
given grains by the owners for the work
done by t~em.
Commodities of daily use are brought
against cash from shopkeepers.
...... The expenditure on clothing i'§ ndt
much because, people use IOGal woven woollen clothes out f'lf home spun wool. A few
Rajput and Thakur families use machine
made cotton cloth during su.mmer. School
children are however seen in cotton clothes
in their school uniforms. Young women
have also developed a taste for cotton
clothes which they put on during summer.
All tllese show a trend for the machine
made clothes.
Expenditure on smoking is incurred by
men only as very few old ladies are seen
smoking hu.kka. Drinking is yet another way
of spending, and large number of people
from all castes are reported to be drinking
wine, such me~ .most~y hail from higl1
castes. At best thlS lS an mdex of expenditure
trends because villagers are ever so vague
about giving this information.

ECONOMY

Village barber charges his wages in cash.
People spend very little on hair cutting.
They cut their hair after a long spell of few
months. Pepple spend little on travel and
spend nothing on medicines. There is a
Government dispensary. They also confine
to their local treatment.
Villagers repay_ old debts which were
for marriage, construction of houses
pr in death rites. On ma'rriages, to keep
their social position they spend lavishly. The
amount of reet paid is also very high. In
one case a Koli incurred 'large .debts
to fight litigation, when he had to face a rich
shopkeeper.
Households who have kept buffaioes spend
little on fodder like khad. More cattle
are also added. Remittance to dependents
are mostly made by Government officials,
and also by one Thakur household who has
sent his son to High School, Rohru. Government officials also pay for the insurance
undertaken by them and also for the G.P.
Fund.
Two shopkeepers pay income tax. Four
shopkeepers and one Government pfficial
spend fair amounts for the servants kept by
them. Shopkeepers have to spend on the
livestock as they are to buy fodder from
villagers against cash payment. Land reve.
nue is paid by 37 households.
Case Study-Income and expenditure
figures for all hpuseholds have been filled
up in schedules. For a case study we may
take up one household of Thakur and Rajput, each' and discuss their income and E;xpenditure. These are just a rough' estimate,
because the replies are vague -and' very confused at times.

No. 1 does little work whereas Nos. 2, 3,

women attending to home work. No.4 reads
in the 10th class in Government High
School, Rohru. The family has sufficient
land, therefore, agricultural labourers are
employed for rabi and kharif harvests. They
do ploughing, sowing, weeding and harvesting. During agricultural days, the average
number pf labourers employed by him is
two. Sometimes about eight labourers are
also engaged in a day. The Thakur informed
that the number of labourers employed by
him during khari} is more than rabi. Besides
giving food grains at two harvests to the
labourers, they are also given (pod. two
times a day during the working days.
Thakur uses his own seeds and old agricultural implements. He reported that he

Education
During 1948, a primary school was opened
and it was upgraded to the middle standard
from 2nd May, 1959. Before 1948, the nEarest
middle school was at Arhal. The high school
was at Rampur. The children from Chergaon used to go-to Arhal for study. No student from Chergaon ever went to the high
school at Rampur or Simla. The thunaering
stream flowing by the side, is noisy but the
children manage a habit. How they do is
anyone's guess. Probably they get used to

The winter vacation for two months begins

Besides, students grow flowers. The children have also started growing some trees on
the banks of the stream and: also around the
school bUildings.
Out of 129 students reading in the school,
24 boys and 7 girls belong to Chergaol). This
includes children pf Government officials
and shopkeepers.
.
The break-up of children caste-wise who
go to school from the village is-

Students play games like kabaddi, Volley
ball-and kushti. They do exercise on the
single bar and parallel bars. Kabaddi is a
favourite game. The compound of the school
is kept fairly neat. Waste paper baskets
made of empty dry milk tins are fixed to the
woodet. poles. The health record of each
student has been maintained. No midday meals are served to the students. During
1961, about 65() Ibs. dry milk was distributed
among the children.

spedes of trees.

Only Primary Health Centre has been
opened. The nearest hospital is at Rohru
where indoor and outdoor medical facilities
are available. The most common diseases
are, dysentery, fever, cold, cough anct influenza. Children sometimes suffer from measles
or chicken pox. Malaria is not so frequent.
The old people felt that this was common
prior to th~ banning on the poppy cultivation. In 1953, Malaria Eradication Scheme
was intr~duced. D.D.T. spraying was started
fr,om 1954 and is being sprayed regularly
during the transmission season between
April and September every year. It was in
1958 that a mass scale programme was
started to eradicate Malaria. One Malaria
Surveillance worker has been posted in
Chergaon. No case of Malaria has come up
recently.

Treatment by Devta
The orthodox people also believe in their
family devta for treatment of diseases.
Whatever their devta suggests, is complied
with at once. The family devta is .called to
the house of the patient acCt)mpanied oy a
party of about fifteen men. A goat is slain
ir.. honour of the devta and the party is entertained with good food. The devta is consulted through the oracle about the disease
and treatment. On devta's suggestion one
more goat is slain in its name. Thus, the
family has to bear more expenditure in this
connection. Only rich families of Thakurs
and Rajputs can afford this type of treatment. Some younger generation, of the village are now loosing faith in treatment by
the devta and openly resent it. An elderly
man, Fina Dass of Bhonda village has reformative views. He believes in devtas, but
wants to remove all evil practices in this
connection. He says that poor must be fed.
from the 'purse of the devta, as these divtas
stand for equality and prosperity and not
for the individual and rich.

Witch Doctor

Jantar Mantar. Of course they also have
a keen faith in Ayurvedic treatment. They
go to Tikkri village three miles away whe~e
a Government Ayurvedic compounder IS
posted. Table No. 48 gives the number of
households who believe in different types of
tre'ltment.

Family structure and Inheritance of Property
Joint family system is the common family
unit. This is approved by social traditions.
Often they comprise of parents and their
~arried sons, their wives and their children,
or of brQthers, their wives and: their children.
It is common for sons, to separate for their
;father e~rly a.fter their ffiflrriage. The villa.-

As a result of division, the sons start a separate family which gradually becomes larger
as more children are added. Again" when
sons grow up, they are married and continue
to live with their parents, when new domestic quarrels take place within the family and
again the division takes place. Thus the
cycle I)f family formatipn anq Q,ivi~lon go~~
on.

SOCIAL AND CULTURAt tIFE
I .

A young married couple· separated from
parents or brothers generally maintains close
relationship and: connection with their family. In the beginning the relations remain
strained. But with time, disputes wear off
and rituals, ceremonies, feasts and festivals
bring them close tagether. The absence of
anyone- is viewed ~eriously.
Within th~ famlly units, the eldest male
is regarded as the head of the family to
whom ·all due respect is shown by other
members. All agricultural operations are
carried out at his guidance. His wife or next
senior male ,is given the second place. The
keys of tl?e house are kept by the wife of
the head .and· all internal household expenditure like food: and bringing of ration from
kuthar are regulated by her through directing her daughters and daughters-in-law.
Side by side, the eldest woman imparts necessary instructions to the daughters-in-law.
Between son.s and daughters, vpreference is
given to former, but in case daughter is
elder, young'er brothers show due respect to

SimpLe fa'l'l'lily-Consists of husband, wife
and lillmarried children.
Intermediate-Married
married brother, sister

Joint-Married coupie with married sons

sisters.
All married women except the headman's
wife, are equal in status and those who bear
children are most liked by the menfolk in
the family. Children are loved by all the
elders.
According to the Hindu Law, sons used
to have full right to inherit the father's
property. Daughters were not given any share
in the property. They were carefully brought
up till thei_r marriage. In a word they used
to get mamtenance allowance ti'll their marriage. After marriage girls had no claim to
farther's property. Traditions lay down that
the aged father should equally divide the property among his sons in his presence. If unfortunately, he is unable to perform the division, property is divided by a senior, near and
dear relative. It was also reported by some of
the elderly people that cases of' brothers
having gone to civil courts or Panchayats for
the division of property have also taken
place.
In case one of the brothers IS minor, he
is looked after either by the aged father or
mother or any of the brothers till he attains
the age of sharing responsibilities. Usually
the parents like to live and pass their last
days with a son to whom they may give
lion's share in the course of d.ivision. Other
brothers do nQt object. If a man dies child~
IQBa, the property goes to his wife. After
her death, it is divided among her husband's
brothers.
In polygamous marriages, the wives have
equal right to their husband's property.
Polyanciry up to great extent, prevents the
further division of the property. In case a
son marries a low caste woman, he' cannot
have a claim to his father's property. Illegitimate sons do not have any claim.
After the enforcement of the act of Hindu
Law of Succession, daughters can also claim
the father's property. One Rajput and one
Thakur family have set up an example by
dividing their property among sons and
daughters equally. At present, the property
divid,ed h'as been entered in the \revenue
records. It is und:er the ownership of the
father but the daughters can have their share
at will. This division took place at the wish
of the father and: not at the instance of the
daughter.s. In no case had daughters desired
to create troubles and pr2SS for their shares.
When they were asked about this legislation.

SOCIAL AND CULTURAL LIFt

villagers felt that this leads to further fragmentation of holdings and creates discord
between brothers and sisters. On second
thought the people felt that it was a good
reform because· like sons, the daughters were
entitled to a share in their father's property.
Out of 68 householcis, 55 households were
aware that there have been change in Hindu
Succession Act.

In the off days, menfolk drink and the
. merry making continues for days. When
local wine is finished, they switch over to the
country liquor. This is a grana way of spending leisure.
Fairs, festivals and social rituals provide
songs and laughters. The young men sometimes play cards. The shops are frequently
visited by the villagers. The two hoteZtvalas
also provide an opportunity to the villagers
to while away their time where they come
across other people of the area and discuss
the matters of common interest. While washing clothes, women steal an hour or half to
have heart's content gossips with their
friends-village women under the shade of
trees.

Status of Women
Women carry out the household work.
They cook, wash clothes, clean utensils and
fetch water. They bring up G_hildren, rear
cattle and collect fuel and fodder. They also
work in the fields. Except ploughing, every
thing else they do in Agriculture. '> .....

During off days from agriculture, she

Leisure and Recreation
After crops have, been sown, villagers
find some time for respite. The slack season
are the winter months when it is severely
cold outsid:e.
Once a while a unit of Himachal Publicity Department gives a play. No dramas are
staged. In the records of Gram Sewak, it has
been written down that a dramatic club
with 20 members was started during 1959.
But no drama has been staged so far. During
the course of investigation an effort was
made by the Government officials and local
persons to stage a drama, but it did not
finalize.

Dance
Men and wo en join into jatra during fairs
ar.d fe~tivals, ' ostly after kharif crops have
been sown. All ages participate and perform
the dance 'by holding hands together and
moving in a circle. The two hands are stretched so that the man or the woman on either
sides holds the hands and a dhait'.-like,.Jater
is made. The steps are taken in uniform way.
Sometimes, the hands are kept on the backs
of the side partners.
The musical instruments played during
the dance are: r~J.r sircgha, karnal, dhol, bam,
nagara, dhamoti, kansol and bhana.

Folk Songs and Fairs
. During jatras, there are folk songs. These
are alternately sung by men and women
from the dancing party. These are also sung
by women while doing agricultural work.
Songs are composed by the villagers whenever any love episode or ar.. interesting legend
comes to light. Trese are composed in rural
tunes. Some of the folk songs are-

First of all mo.on shines and then stars

as the tomorrow may never come.

Gist-On the ridge of Kalkapattam mountain, people from villages Khabbal and J abbal take their goats for grazing. Usually, the
villagers from these two villages quarrel
over the grazing pasture as they claim it.
During one of these quarrels, one shepherd,
named Vaziioo of Khabbal was killed. The
man who killed Vaziroo was later on arrested by the police and was handcuffed.
Fairs and Festivals
These form a joyful part of the village cultural life. This brings social unity among
different shades of people. We mention a few
fairs and festivals here.
FESTIVALS

Bissu_':'This is a festival celebrated on the
1st of Baisakh. Preparations begin two days
before. Women get busy in washing clothes
and cleaning houses. A thin rope of bhang
is made in which red Rhoaodendron flowers
'are fixed. In the morning, the rope is tied
around the house and little fire is burnt at
the four corners where Puja is performed.
By ~oing so, all harmfu~ germs are supposed
to dIe. Cattle are fed WIth badu grass mixed
with salt a~d are tend~d for grazing in the
early morn mg. SometImes, the fodder is

69

SOCiAL A~D CULTURAL LIFE

charmed so that no casualty occur to their
cattle during the year. Fafra grass is given
to cattle as a medicine against itching.
Shakdi, p02du, masoda and rJce are cooked.
Some make halwa. Cooked rice and other
preparations are sent ,to the married daughters and to near relatives. This is known as
kiLti. The village KoLts and Turis visit the
houses of Thakurs and Rajputs in groups.
They sing and shower blessings. They ~re
given one rupee, a piece of cloth and .grams.
Family pandit and other village memal.s receive grains and food. In case a male ~hlld IS
born in ai family on this day, a goat IS slam
to mark the occasion. Local distilled wine is
a must in dinner and merry makings go on
fbr two or three days.
Dhaknol-This festival is celebrated on the
first of Sawan when paddy transplantation
work is nearly over. People put on clean
clothes and prepare delicious food lik~ poldu
and haLwa. A ceremony is observed. VIllagers
make small images of cattle out of kneaged
flour. In the afternoon these images are taken
to an open ground near Cl)alala village.
Women and children also accompany men.
The people from nearby villages als,? come
with, some images. 13efore sunset, the elders
place the images on the ground and the children beat them with sticks amidst cheers and
shouts and the festival is over.
Chador-Is celebrated on the first of
Bhadra Worship of family devta iH periorm~
ed by burning some fire over a flat stone kept
on a log of mud. This festival is also celebrated by Kolis.
Pular-This is celebrated on any Sunday
of Asauj in honour of Andevta. Eyery~me
carries some food to the most fertIle field
and cook morning meals there. After WOfshipping the Ande'vta, the meals consisting
of poldu are served to the fam~ly members
in the field. They say the food IS very dellcious.
Diwaoli I-First Diwali is celebrated on
the same day as in other parts of the country. The people put on nea and clean clothes
and prepare good dishes.
In the evening, divas are lighted in the
houses and goddess Laxmi is worshipped. A
queer way of concluding the festival is that
after puja, people gather outside in an open
space around a burning fire where they shout
and call names. This custom is followed not
because of any bias against any person but
as an item of their old tradition in which no
scuffles take place.
Diwali II-This is held after a month from
the date of 1st Diwali and is called Burhi

Diwali in local dialect. On this day no lights
are held and in the evening delicious food
like meat, po2du, badey are made. Close relatives are invited to dinner. Wine is also
served. Kiltis are sent to daughters and nieces
who cannot attend the occasion. In the
night, the custom of shouting and abusing is
also there.
Magh-ka-Saja-Celebrated on the 1st of
Magh for which preparations begin two
days earlier. This festival has the great significance of making delicious local food and are
sent to their married daughters, sisters and
nieces in kiZtas. If preparations are not sent
to or received from near relatives, it is a bad
omen. Usually, families expect kitti upto
fifteenth of Magh. Wine is served in plenty
and continues for five or six days.

Shiv-Ratri-In the month of Phalgun, this
festival is celebrated in honour of Lord
Shiva. Usual food like poldu, shakdi, masoday are prepared.
In the morning, the family members take
their 'bath and put un new clothes. Big rotis
called rots are also made of flour which weigh
from one seer to 10 seers each. A room is
white washed and cow dung applied to floor.
In the corner all rots along with other house
preparations are placed in decoration. Upon
the big rot, goats, rams made out of kneaded
flour are nicely ornamented and placed on
there. Image of. Lord Ganpat, is made out of
cowdung and placed in the centre. Kesar, kungoo, and flowers are applied to Ganpat's
forehead. Some Rajput families also make
images of Parvati and keep it alongwith Ganpat. About one foot long garland is made out
of green leaves of bhar maili flowers, Kubash
and barley. Wild citrus fruits locally known
as kimtoo are also placed in the gar land. This
, garland~., known as Mahadeva and is hung
in the centre over the preparations. Puja is
held in the evening when all members of the
house sing devotional songs. Some elder members of the house fast and food is served
after the puja. Meat is an important item
of food on this day. In some houses goats
are slain.
For the village Badhi it is the biggest festiyear. He celebrates it a day later
WIth greater pomp and show. The quantity
of the preparation is also more. After puja the
villagers visit his house and sing devotional
songs throughout the night in praise of Lord
Shiva. Some rich families present some
money like one anna or two, to Mahad.eva
called iSS'IJ,T by Badhi community.
