SOCIAL AND CULTLTRAL LIrE
FAIRS

No fair is held in Chergaon. A brief mention of fairs held near the village are:Rohru Fair-This is held on the 9th and
10th of Baisakh each year in Rohru to celebrate the birth of Rohru devta. The date for
the celebration of this fair was fixed by a
pandit of devta many years ago. A brief
legend about Rohru devta is worth mentioning here. It goes that about a century
ago, there were about 160' households in village Gangtoli. In summer, the villagers used
to tend their cattle, sheep and goats on Chansal peak. Among them was a man who had
a mastery over the flute. Once he threw' bis
flute decorated with Nesar flowers into river,
Pabber with the wish that it should reach
his wife. The flute in the course of its flow
reached Shikri stream below the present
Rohru bridge and one day it came into the
pitcher of his wife who was fetching water.
Seeing the flute of the man, people belieyed
that the husband of that woman had died,
and the flute was carried by Shikr.u devta
believed to be son of Kali goddess.
After this incident, some people of the village began to die of various diseases. A Brahman named Para told that the devta was
angry with the people. The Brahman jumped
in the Shikri stream below bridge and remained under water for a week. After that he appeared with an image of a god and some
gold. He worshipped the deity for some days
and then handed over it to the people of
Rohru who started honouring it by way of
celebrating the said fair annually.
About 8,000 people gather in this fair.
Large number of people from Chergaon in
colourful dresses and gay faces visit the
fair. J atras are held and wine forms the
major item of those who have a craze for
it. About fifty temporary stalls consisting of
various articles for sale used to be installed
by the people. Many goats are slaughtered.
Four merry-go rounds, locally called chadol
are fixed. Its charges are 16 rounds for six
Paise. Government institutions exhibit their
stalls depicting their achievements. Cinema
show is given by the Publicity Department.
Laila fair-Is held on the 21st and 22nd of
Sawan, at Laila 4 miles on the left bank of
Pabber. This is celebrated in honour of Bhim,
one of the five Pandavs. The legend goes that
once Bhim was suffering from leprosy. His
brother Sahadev, after consulting pandit told
Bhim that he could be cured if he caught
hold of Kedar calf who was alike other calves of the village but had some supreme
powers. Bhim was told that the calf could

be distinguished as it does not eat salt. But
Bhim could not distinguish it. Bhim was
again told that the said calf will not pass
under his legs. Bhim stretched his legs over
two hill cliffs and the Kedar calf did not pass
through his legs. He atonce recognised it and
ran after the calf. This proved like a wild
goose chase. It was with utmost difficulty
that Bhim was able to catch it near" Kedarnath. Thus Bhim recovered from lep'rosy
and the fair is celebrated in his honour.
Five devtas of Dali, Gawas, Diswani, Astani and Chopari villages come to the fair.
During State regime" free ration to the devta's party was given on behalf of the ruler,
but now devta's own kothar meets to this requirement. About two thousand people gather
in the fair from the surrounding villages.
. Many persons come from Chergaon.
About twenty small stalls are put up in this
fair. Mostly these consist of tea and cigarettes. Merry-go-rounds are also there. Men and
women gather dressed in, new and colourful
clothes and dance jatras and sing. The best
jewellery is put on by women on this fair.
To maintain law and order in the fair, a
couple of police-men are provided by the
Rohru Police Station. Wine is not taken during daytime but is served after sunset. People
return to their villages next day.
Devidhar fair-Is held in the memory of
a sadhu about 8 miles away and the gathering
is about 3,000. The legend goes that there was
a Thakur family in Ransar valley who had no
children. He undertook the pilgrimage to
Mansrowar lake in the Himalayas to have a
child. There are two big lakes named M antalab and Rakshas talab. Thakur after taking
bath in Mantalab started cooking food. A
noble spirit living in the Mansrowar lake appeared before Thakur in the disguise of a
sadhu and learnt about the delicious food of
Ransar Valley. The'spirit wanted to accompany the Thakur. Hesitatingly, the Thakur
agreed and both of them started on the return
journey. In the midway, Thakur thought of
getting rid of sadhu and hid himself. Bewildered, the sf1,dhu moved on and reached Badhi
range. There he saw that an evil spirit was
forCibly getting agricultural work from 136
men. On seeing the evil spirit the sadhu
beat him with kungshi-poisonous plant.
Next day the people of that area were happy
to f..ila that the devil was killed by the
sadhu. The people were thus relieved of their
miseries inflicted by the evil spirits. They
give honour to the sadhu by celebrating
this fair at Devidhar.
One devta of Devidhar village is present
in the fair. People gather in new and ~olour.
ful dresses. A few merry-go-rounds and

SOCIAL AND CULTURAL LIFE

about ten to fifteen tiny shops crop up. Many
jatars are held.
Some interesting fairs are held at Guma,
Kupad, Bhampad and Intali villages.
A feature of fairs in this illaqa is, that
women and young girls give parched wheat
and rice or modi to their relatives, friends and
to outsiders. In return they receive cash gifts
from elders. The women carry bags of modi
for distributing it among acquai~tances.
J agra-It is a night festival where every
one keeps awake villages of rejoices ...... and
is held in the nearby villages of Sondha,
Chalala, Tjkkri, Khashdhar. Many from
Chergaon join. The investigator had the occasion of attending jagras at Sondha and
Chalala villages. These are held on dooj teej
and chauth in the Krishna pakash of Bhadon
month. Any of the three days can be chosen
to celebrate jagras to honour Mahasu devta's
birthday.
.
'
Villagers from the surrounding villages
gather and sit around an open place meant
for the jagra. Some people occupy the
verandahs of the surrounding houses. The
local devta is worshipped at about 9.00 P.M.
The dev',ta's priest worships Chida-an
image on a flat stone balanced on a wooden
beam. The image is made out of wheat flour
and kungu. The people and village devta
witness the puja ceremony amidst the beating of drums and playing of musical instruments. Some young men carry khukries .in
their hands. During puja, a goat is slaughterp
ed and the head offered at Chida. The young
men at once begin to dance with khukries
in their hands' and devta is also carried to
dance. The oracle also dance under de'vta's
influence. The dance continues for a while.
Then it takes the form of a regular jatra.
The devta's oracle and the priest first start
the jatra. Their places are then taken by
other influential people. A Turan also dances
at the head of the jatra. She belongs to
Turi scheduled caste. The dance continues
till the break of dawn and is wound up by a
dance of oracle and Turan. Free food is
given to the poor at night. The fire 'on Chida
IS kept burning throughout the night. A regular feature during devta's puja is that the
oracle pierces his cheek or tongue with a
thin needle. This is done under the influence
of the devta. But there is no wound.
In a jagra held at Sondha an account of
an incident may be mentioned here. A young
girl while dancing in jatra was talking with
her new boy friend. Her previous young lover
could not see the affair. Accompanied by
friends, he dragged away the girl and gave
her a good beating. None of the people pre-

71

sent in the jagra interfered in the affair and
the jagra went on as usual. Some Government officials tried to interfere, but were
threatened to remain away from the affair.
Not an un-common sight.
In jagra at Khashdhar, two marriages of
dhara type took place during that night. Both
belonged to swarn castes.
Bhoj-It is a fair of. Kolis. A bhoj was held
near Sandasu about two miles away on the
27th Manghar. Many small devtas of Kolis
from different village gathered.
Bhoonda-Bhoonda is a festival of Brahmins, like shant which is a festival of Rajputs
and bhoj of Kolis. In bhoonda other castes
participate to make it a success. In this festival, a man from a Beda caste is made to slide
down a rope stretched 'between a cliff down
to its bottom. It is held after twelve, fifteen
or twenty years in villages; Nagar, Samarkot,
Samrairi, Bachhunchh, Spail; Nandla, Kulgaon, Sholi and Kharahan of former Bushahr State and in Nirmand in Kulu. The investigator has some faint memories of a bhoonda seen in Nagar which has held sometimes about 21 years bac~, when a man was
successfully lowered down the rope. In this
festival, hundreds of people from all wal~s
of life had gathered and many tiny shops
were run. The Beda had two little red flags
in his out stretched hands and his legs were
tied with gunny bags full of sand to maintain the balance over the rope. His wife and
'children were sitting at the lower end and
were praying. Many devtas were brought
to Nagar. After he got to the end, the Beda
was carried on the shoulders to the temple
amidst great rejoicing and music.
After independence, Government banned
any person from being lowered down the
rope. To complete the ceremony, now a goat
is lowered down-a substitute for a man.
Dialect
Villagers speak Chuharoo dialect when
they talk to each other. This is the principal
dialect of the whole valley. While talking to
an outsider, the villagers manage to speak
Hindi. In Rohru Tehsil four other dialects
are spoken. These are the dialects of Dodra
Kawar, Nawari, Mandalgarhi and Rohru
areas. Two pieces from Ramayana translated in Chuharoo dialect are given at appendix
No. VI.
Religion
In Chergaon mostly Hindus live. There is
no temple of its own in the village and people
worship Jabbal devta known as Jabbal
Narain. Some of them have a small image of

72
it kept in their houses or in kothcirs. Devta
plays a predominant part in social and ritual
practices of the people.
The younger generation has little interest
in religion and its ritual activities. Rich families perform worships and rituals in honour
of their family devta. They also worship many
other Hindu gods like Lord Krishna, Ram,
Vishnu, Lakshmi and Shiva.
The elderly people have old beliefs which
revolve round the doctrine of karma. Some
are often heard to remark; "we must submit
to what has been written in our fate. Human
efforts cannot alter the will of God".
Caste-system also plays its own role and'
specifies that certain things must not be done
by certain castes which may lead to impurity
of their religion. For an example, inter-dining
with a lower caste person is against the
dharma of a swarn.
Besides J abbal devta, Kalis also worship
their devta known as Kilbalu-ka-devta. This
devta has tracked to this part of the Tehsil
via Kalka-pattam from Kilba a village in
Kinnaur. There was a proud Koli named
Bantoo who nad seven sons. All the daughters-in-Iaw of Koli Bantoo were turned barren by the devta. Other Kolis did not recognise the devta also. Therefore, devta through
his powers, turned all bulls and cows unserviceable. This created difficulties for the
Koli community. The people were compelled to recognise and worship the Kilbalu
devta.
Untouchability
Koli is a lower caste community doing
agricultural labour for the high class. Jobs
like leather work, tanning and flaying is
done by some of the people. Untouchability
is deep rooted in the social life of the people
and the swarrc's keep the distance and show
their taboos in many respects. The reason
put forward for this stiff attitude by elderly
persons is that Koli,s dispose off the dead
cattle and unclean them. Some went up to
the extent of giving different eye witness account of the matter. Swarns have always
dominated over this class. Cases of Kolis
being beaten up by the Thakurs have also
come to notice. In one of two such cases, a
Thakur mercilessly beat a Koli boy simply
because he did not give him a lantern. Other
cases are there. Kolis have little land and
feel tightly for houses to live in. The upper
castes have always kept Kolis under their
thumb. They get things done according to
their desire.
Untouchability Act seems to have no meaning. Swam's attitude is deeply rooted in certain traditional conventions, like not inter-

SOCIAL AND CULtURAL LIt:E!

dining or fetching water from the same
spring. Out of 68 households, 45 households
know about Untouchability Act. Kolis are
not permitted to enter the temples in this
part. Till recently, the Brahmans of Chergaon
were very orthodox. If one comes across a
Koli in the journey, he would talk to him
through a stone lying in the road. The Koli
would at-,once move to the left side of the
Brahman and allow him to pass. Koli also
avoids that his shc:dow may fall on the Brahman.
Tatooing
The women are fond' of tatooing different
parts of their body. The craze for this begins
when they are still little girls. Tatooing is
done in their own local method. Half riped
baFley ears are taken and ground to make a
~hm ,Paste. ?ome black soot from a frying p'an,
IS ~llxe~ WIth the paste. The mixture is pricked m dIfferent parts of the 'body with a thorn
of bhaikhli shrub. A rough sketch is always
made out of a pencil on the body before tatooing. Some girls write their names and some
like flowers or birds. The wound heals within
three or four days. A little mustard oil is
used. The parts of the body tatooed are forearms, lower portion of legs, breasts and sometimes chins, cheeks or forehead.
Very few elderly men keep long hair except the central portion of the head. The
people told that this was a old custom and
compare this to ,Lord Krishna who had long
hair.
Common Habits
In summer, people g~t up at about 5.30 A.M.
and go to bed at 9.30 P.M. In winter the hours
are 6.30 A.M. and 11.00 P.M. Some men sleep
in summer afternoons. Women do not have
any time. They are busy in one work or the
other.
At one time this part was an opium growing area. A few people used to take opium.
Opium cultivation is banned now and no one
takes it.
Men smoke hukka or cigarettes bidies. It
is not common among women except a few
old ladies. Men like a drink but not women.
Among Harijans, there is a common habit
while taking wine. Whenever a Harijan is
offered wine by a party of swarns, the former bows in respect .and utter Ram-Ram
to the party, before he drinks the wine. He
takes the wine in his hands and not in cup
or tumbler before the party.
A woman gree~s another by bowing. Her
two hands almost touch the feet. The other

SOCIAL AND CULTURAL LIFE

woman acknowledges the greetings by raising
her hands upto breast, praying for her and
her husband's long life. The greeting is initiated by a younger or a Harijan woman.
Menstruation period i;; also considered an
impure period for three days both among
swarns and Harijans. The woman does not
cook nor does she fetch water or enter the
kitchen. She attends to outdoor work ..
There is one common practice among the
people. They call young girls or women and
young boys by suffixing the word "ti" and
'tu'. For example, the names of Krishana,
Sheela and Vidya-all women are called as
Krishnati, Sheelati and Vidyati. Sohan Lal
and Gian Chand are called as Sohantu or
diantu. This is an act of love and affection.
One more practice among the villagers is
that goats are sometimes slaughtered on roofs
during festivals and f~irs.
Beliefs and Superstitions
People have deep rooted faith in various
beliefs and superstitions.
The Supreme God is Jabal-ka-devta. All
rituals and customs connected with their
birth, marriage, death, house construction
and others are directed by the wish of the
devta through its oracle. Even the out-break
of any epidemic and big disputes are referred to the devta.
About three decades back, people of Garhwal and this area used to graze their sheep
and goats over dandas which form the boundary between former Bushahr State and
Garhwal. Some men of Chuhara valley
stole away goats and sheep of the other
party. On hearing the news, the ruler of
Bushahr imprisoned some of his own men
just to teach them a lesson. Immediately
after, the ruler fell ill and inspite of the
best medical aid, he could not recover.
J abbal devta was held in high esteem by
the ruler also, so he sent for the devta. The
devta agreed to visit him provided all his
men were first set free. The ruler agreed and
devta cured him completely. The ruler was
so pleased that he permitted devta to collect
the revenue of the two villages amounting to
Rs. 500/-.
About half. a century back, one Lohar used
to live in Chergaon. He married a woman of
Dhagoli village few miles away. At the time
of marriage, she was suffering from plague
and died immediately after her marriage.
While cremating her, a notorious man named
Shoba Singh removed a dhagloo-a forearm
ornament and the infection spread. He also
died of plague. Plague spread il1 th~ villa~e

73

and people began to die. They ran away and
lived in the jungles. The village remained
uninhabited for about a month. It was Jabbal
devta who cleared plague and people returned to their homes.
Sick people get cared after making promises and gifts to the devta. Even if a son/
grandson or daughter is born in a swam.
household, the devta visits alongwith a party
of ten or fifteen persons to pour congratulations. Food is served to the party for about
two days at the cost of household. Some cash
presents are also made.
A trend noticed among youngmen is that
they do not respect the old beliefs so much
now. Recently, Dharam Singh. Rajput married his daughter to Gian Singh, Panchayat
Chowkidar. After some-time Gian Singh
turned put his wife and remarried. The first
wife fell very sick. On this, Dharam Singh
sent for a local devta of nearby village and
entertained the party for two nights. Under
the devta's influence, its oracle said that
Gian Singh had taken the help of an evil
spirit. When the oracle was entering the
house of Gian Singh, he refused entry and
the oracle returned. The whole matter ended
in a fuss.
Devta's path should never run below the
level of Harijan's houses. In Sondha village,
devta is carried through a path made in fields
because some houses of Kolis are at higher
level. They say that devta's path is always
high.
A strange way of settling a dispute over a
stolen property exists in the village even now.
Suppose a stolen goat is recovered from a person, both the parties who claim the goat, go
to temple to decide the matter. Goat is
kept before the devta and in the devta's
name, the rightful owner removes his goat.
The thief does not speak a ,word for fear of
devta's curse. Similarly decisions over stolen
properties are also taken.
There is a peculiar belief regarding solar
or lunar eclipses. Whenever either of the
eclipses takes place, the people begin to call
names to Rahu or Kaitu. They shout like
chhod papiya chhod meaning 'Oh rascal leave
them'. According to them, either of the two
is supposed to devour the sun or moon during
an eclipse. People also give grains in charity
to the Brahmans. During the eclipse, no food
is cooked or taken. After the eclipse is over,
people wash their utensils and cook fresh
meals. In some houses, head and horns of a
goat are fixed to the main door. They say
this prevents entry of evil spirit. If two
snakes are seen together a piece of cloth is
spread over them. Thi~ is done SQ that no ill

Village Organisation land Leadership
Before the accession of Bushahr State, villagers were organized under different village
leaders ,nominated by the ruler of the State.
Selection of such leadership depended upon
territory, caste and religion of the man and
the posts were hereditary. For territory, a
man should hail from a particular place who
could command respect from the vast area
just to facilitate the State work. Similarly, religion and caste also played a prominent role
because a man belonging to a high caste
would be able to organise the people in a
better way.
Z.aildar-The highest post of the village
organisation in the past was of a Zaildar
who was incharge of a pargna comprising
of some group of villages. His main function used to maintain the law and order in
his area. If any epidemic breaks out, he used
to inform the ruler. All arrangements were
made by him in connection with the visit of
any distinguished person to his village. The
level of the State's coffer was maintained by
him by way of even resorting to compulsory
collection. In return, he had certain privileges. He did not pay any land revenue. Some
land was also given to him for cultivation by
the ruler. Free services were offered to him
by people of all shades.
Lambardar-The next in command of the
village organisation is a village lamberdar.
His primary duty is to collect land revenue
and deposit it in the Government treasury
for which he is paid five percent of the total
collection. The intermediary agency between
lamberdar and Government is a village patwari. In Chergao,n, they have no lamberdar
and the village falls under the lamberdari of
Cheloti
village. The post is hereditary.
.
.

75
Mate-The mate of the. village. is a village
servant who is paid a fixed salary Rs. 13/P.M. His function is to attend to Government officials and help them in discharging
Governm~nt duties. The present mate is very
tactful, WItty ard helpful. This is not a hereditary post.

Halbandi-A halbandi was a village servant and belonged to Harijan community.
The halbandi was supposed to do all work
free of charge as desired by zaildar or lamberdar. This was known as begar. If grains
are to be carried from Rohru to Rampur
the zaildar would depute half a dozen haZ~
bandis from each village.
In . t~e past, there was village panchayat
conslstmg of e!derly and influential people.
!hey settle? dIsputes between people hailmg from dIfferent castes. The disputes used
to be of minor nature like trespassing into
another's fields. It saved their time and
~oney ~nd the inconve~ience of appearing
II?- State s court. SometImes, the parties at
dIspute come to blows. To settle these disputes, the village elders used to play a very
significant part. These were known as pan..
ches. They used to be in odd number and
were men who were respected. Their verdict
was respected and accepted. In case, any
person or party tried to challenge the verdict, they were subjected to the imposition
of social s~nctions, like hukapani band
karna. There was no caste panchayat among
Harijans. If any minor dispute cropped up,
that was settled by themselves. While selecting village panches, caste was one of the
considerations. Kolis were always deprived
of such organisation. They were always
dominated by Rajputs and Thakurs.
Panchayat
The village Gram Panchayat with headquarters at Chergaon was established in 1954.
It covers 22 villages with a total population
of 2,677 persons, having 1,560 voters.
In all there are 17 members; 5 belong to
Scheduled Castes and 3 are women. One
male member represents the vi11age in Gram
Panchayat. Members of Gram Panchayat are
known as Panches elected by votes of Gram
Sabha. Its President and Vice-President
known as Pradhan and Up-Pradhan are elected through direct election.
Activities of Gram Panchayat cover development of Panchayat circle, repairs of paths
~nd cleaning of water sources, The Panchayat

SOCIAL AND CULTURAL LIFE

76

settles disputes among different persons. It
keeps a record ot births and dea~hs of all vi~lages in the circle, regulates faIrs and festIvals and collect tax from stall-holders' and
shopkeepers in the fairs. The Investigator observed the 'Panchayat of Kaloti an adjoining
circle, collecting stall-tax during'Laila fair.
Meetings are held every month to review the
position. Sometimes extraordinary meetings
are also called by giving three days notice to
t.he members.
A N ayaya Panchayat has been established.
Members are known as Panches and the
President and Vice-President as Sarpanch
and Naib-sarpanch. The members are elected
by direct votes, indirect election is ,held for
Sarpanch and Naib-sarpanch. One swarn
member from Chergaon represents the Nayaya Panchayat having 15 members. There is"'?
Harijan Panch in this body.
.
Civil as well as criminal cases are taken by
Nayaya Panchayat and trials are conducted
when a case comes up. Fines are imposed.
Some may refus~ to pay them. Their cases
are referred to the Magistrate Rohru for the
recovery.
To settle disputes, Pradhan nominates a
negotiation committee of 3 to 5 Panches which
attempts to bring a compromise among the
parties. Failing this, Pradhan also makes a
personal attempt to bring the partiesi close.
Similarly in Nayaya Panchayat, 3 negotiation committees have been made. A case is
referred to any of the committees. If their
efforts fail, the case is referred to a full
bench of Nayaya Panchayat leaving aside
Sarpanch. One member each for the aggrieved and the aggressor to represent their cases
from N ayaya Panchayat, is allowed. Votes
are taken by show of hands.
18 cases in respect of the village came up
before the Panchayat out of which 15 were
civil cases. Ten provisions dealing with the
civil cases, fall under the jurisdiction of
Gram Panchayat. Some of the important
cases brought up before the Pahchayat are1. Two parties quarrelled over a tree standing on the boundary of the two fields. One
party belonged to Chergaon and the 2nd
party belonged to Koti village. The case was
taken up under Section 426, by the Nayaya
Panchayat during 1960. The parties even
came to blows over the ownership of the tree.
The parties were 'brought to compromise and
the tree was divided equally among them.

2. In another case, a married man turned
out his wife as she was not bearing good
character. The wife filed an application
daimin~ tn9-iJ:ltena,nce illlQwa.nce before the

Nayaya Panchayat. Under Section 488, the
Panchayat tactfully handled the case~ Neither
of the parties were in a mood for reconciliation. Ultimately the two were united and
they are now leading a happy life.

.3. In another case a man' threatened to kill
another man when some minor quarrel arose
between them. The aggrieved filed em application before Nayaya Panchayat. Under Section 107, it took up the case for hearing. As
the aggrieved man could not produce the
witness, the case was dismissed.
Except two, all the other 13 civil cases are
in connection with r.ural indebtedness. The
debtor could not pay the money and the
money-lender filed suit against him in the
Panchayat. All cases were settled by decree!ng the debtor. Two cases are of the interestmg nature. In one case, a family owed some
grains to devta. The manager of that devta
'filed a suit against him. The case was taken
up by the Panchayat during 1961, but no decision has been arrived at so far. In another
case, one man stole a new pair of shoes worth
Rs. 15 belonging to another person. The
man filed an application with the Panchayat.
A decree for Rs. 15 was passed against the
accused.
A Secretary has been apPointed to keep accounts of Panchayat, record proceedings and
convene the meetings. He. is paid Rs. 50
P.M. Now scales have been revised to Rs. 604-80. One chaukictar-cum-peon has also been
appointed at Rs. 20 P.M. Both of them belong to Chergaon.

election held on the 30th August, 1962. In all

There was a great interest on the election
day. Large number of voters came to cast
their votes. The eight circle-wise seats were

election. There was a keen contest for the
seat of Pradhan. At first there were 4 candidates. One withdraw and three candidates
were left in the field. A Rajput youth was
declared elected who polled 160 votes.
The counting is done by raising of hands.
In the election of Pradhan, the candidates
were made to stand at a distance of a yard
apart and the voters were asked to stand behind the person of their choice. Before election, the candidates were convassing their
voters and distributing free cigarettes. The
highlight of the election was that one woman
aged 90 and a dumb Thakur also exercised
franchise.
When people were asked about separate
panchayats for different communities, they
expressed different opinions. Swarns showed
that they were contented with the fu.nctions
of the present Parichayat and did not want
any division in village community. Harijans

on the other hand remained silent and did
not breath so freely. They grumbled and
appeared unhappy with the present Panchayat. In fact, they are landless. They feel
that their own separate Panchayat may do
some good in uplif.ting their economic position.
For any suggestions towards improvements
in the Panchayat, people have nothing to express. They say that it is functioning well. No
harm has been done to any person and all
major or small disputes are settled in the village. Uptill now, no appeal has been filed
against Panchayat's judgement. During the
past ten years, only eleven criminal cases
reached Police Station, Rohru, out of \·.hich
5 were of theft, two of illicit distillation and
one each of criminal assault on a girl, unauthorised possession of land, keeping of an
unlicensed gun and an armed clash between
two parties. Table No. 52 shows some information about main functiol)s of Panchayat.

Co-operative Society
Rama Co-operative Multipurpose Society,
Chergaon was started on the 13th October,
1951. It covers 22 villag'2s compriSing 2,500
members.
Capital

The present financial position of the society
isShare Capital

The maximum credit limit of the society is
Rs. 3,000.
M embers and Share
Membership is open to all. One can become
a member by buying a share of Rs. 10 and
by depositing Re. 1 as an admission fee.
Under the Act, the maximum number of
shares that a member can have is either one
fifth of total paid up share capital or Rs. 10,000
whichever is less. There are 52 members out
of which 23 members belong to Chergaon.
They have a total capital of Rs. 600 as their
share. The maximum two shares of Rs. 100
each have been purchased ,by two Thakur
brothers.
Liability-In case the society fails, liabilities on members are four times the subscribed share capital.
Purpose-At present, tpe aim of the society
is to supply consumers articles to the members at cheap rates. No loans are advanced
by the Society at present. With the advancing of loans, it will hamper the importance of
money-lenders and reduce the market rate
of interest.
Co-operative shop-A Co-operative shop has
been startedi in a rented house for Rs. 5 P.M.
The former salesman did not work honestly
and embezzled amounts. Very few articles
are seen in the shop. The managing committee can change the salesman if. his work is not
satisfactory. We gather he is not paid and is
given 50 per cent of the profit as his share.,
Attitude towards the society-Villagers do
not understand nor appreciate the objects and
aims of the society. One more reason, more
than any other is lack of proper publicity,
complexity of. Government rules and structUre of behaviour. They take it a cheap agency
organised by Government officials and some
inftuencial villagers. Their activities are
viewed with doubt. Members do not take interest in the society. The present society can
-run: the shop at a better standard and we are
sure one day, it can give relief to people by
selling commodities at cheaper rates. Some
at Chergaon have been requesting to the
managing committee to run the shop better.
The present shop remains closed for number
of days together. If all dhOop is marketed by
the Society it can give promising dividends to
members and also to Dhoop-owners. Its
nature of not advancing loans for agricultural
and other purposes is another worry of the
people. Farmers are compelled to approach
money-lenders for taking loans at high rate
of interest,

v. Conclusion
Since independence, there had been many
significant changes. What was a remote area
of Bushahr state, is now accessible area and
developing into a prosperous part of the
Pradesh. There were no means of communication it took nearly 6 to 7 days to get to
Rohru on a horse or on foot. Now one gets
there in a few hours.
Forced labour-begar, which at one time
was required, no longer exists. Big landlords
whose autihority was supreme and unchallenged in the past, are now loosing their
popularity. With the set up of Panchayat
people exercise franchise according to their
desire. Cases are decided by the Panchayat
within the village without any distinction of
class or creed. Village Co-operative Society
now running, will have further improvement by the passage of time. With the establishment of a school in 1948, people are gradually realising the benefits of education.
Prior to 1948, the people had to contend with
the learning of tankars script from a pandit. A primary health centre is also functioning which provides medical facilities.
Before the start of a vehicular road upto
Chergaon more than 95 per cent of the
people had not seen an automobile. A post
office now functioning in the village, was a
dream in the past.

Changes in the relationship of the village
to the world outside also became increasingly significant after the independence. Shops
and eating houses appeared in the village
where people of different castes mix up
and talk to each other.
The change in the people's dress, ornaments and manners are observed. The use of
shirt and pyjama has become common
among the youngsters. Even the women
have been seen using pyjama made of cotton
cloth instead of traditional woollen suthan.
The heavy and old: ornaments are not
used by young women. They prefer light
a.nd new fashioned jewellery. There is a continuous expansion of Government institutions, system of transport, communication
and trade. The people have become political
conscious as well and know to exercise their
franchise for selecting a suitable representative.
About a decade back, there were a few
government (Jfficials posted, but now more
than a score of Government officials representing different activities of the government are posted here. Villagers do not feel
shy and mix with officials.

81

As a single unit the village commands the
loyalty of all who live in it irrespective of
their affiliation to different castes. Villagers
life revolves round the pivot of unity and
co-operation within a certain frame of caste
without which it is difficult for an individual to lead an isolated life. On festivals,
people work together to make the occasion
a success. Even in agricultural operations,
people help Each other of the time of need
and in most cases the agricultural implements are also borrowed. The work on social
sides like road construction. cleaning of
hawaIi or repairs of kuhl are undertaken on
collective basis.
Caste structure has a close tie with the
village economics. Different castes are
,pecialized in different trades. The noncultivating castes or households provide certain special services to the cultivators and
rpceiv€ foodstufi as their wages. The quanhty of food stuff received, depends upon
various factors like economic position of the
cultivator and the work done by the craftsman. It also depends upon the qualification
of the craftsman in a particular occupatiJm.
\. relationship between two differ~nt .occupational da3ses is a product of obllgatlOn and

CONCLUSION

gives each of them a claim over the other.
The system of reciprocal obligation on give
and take basis in the village is known as
zhakhri kamana.
1JD)ty 1'0. ahD D'oSel'Ven 'm 'their social liTe.
The house structures of the villagers are uniform, they wear the same type of clothes
and ornaments. The utensils used are of
s!milar type. They speak one dialect irrespective of caste and community. All practise
the same. old technique in agriculture.
Whether nch or poor, unity in all social
functions is apparently the same. Their
birth, marriage and death rites are broadly
similar.

The study shows that 65 married women
living in the village came from 49 different
villages at a distance ranging up to 35 miles,
leavmg aside the bania families. If we take
the daughters of Chergaon who are married
outside the village, there will be about the
same number of daughters married, in Chergaon from other villages. Thus little unit
of 68 households becomes the centre of kinship bonds of 49 villages. It makes a small
comI?unity of .Chergaon to a bigger commUlllty of the lllaqa imbibing many similar
aspectf. of life.

English Versiol1
RAMA'S ADVICE TO LAXMANA
Lord Rama did not agree to Laxmana's
accompanying him on exile, He told that
only those who obeyed their, parents and
preceptor have succe,eded in life, Therefore
you should act upo~ my advice and serve
the parents, Bharata and Shatrughana are
not at home, father is old and he is worried
about me, In such circumstances if I take

APPENDICES

fOll with me for exile, Ayodhya will be a
deserted town. Preceptor, father, mother,
people and family will be in trouble. Therefore you stay here and keep them happy.
In case you do J¥)t stay it will be a sin. In
whose reign people are not happy, the ruler
definitely deserves hell.
Hearing this Laxmana was perplexed. He
was so sad as lotus becomes pale by the
hoar frost.
Perplexed Laxmana lowered on the feet
of Rama and: said, "Lord, I am a small child
brought up with your affection. I dtJ not
know father, mother and any body else except you".
English version
RAM RAJYA
When Rama established his reign, the
whole world became happy. In Rama's reign,

nobod~ had malice. All the people according
to then' caste and religious order, were devotees of their religions and followed the
Vedas. They neither feared nor worried nor
suffered: from any disease. People loved each
other. Cult was based on truth. bravery,
sympathy and charity. All men and women
were devotees pi Rama. Death at early age
was nowhere and nobody was oppressed.
People had beautiful looks and enjoyed
good health. All men and women were generous and benevolent. Men were attached
to one wife only. Similarly wpmen were
well wishers of their husbands from the core
of their hearts, words and actions. In the
forest, trees we're always blessed: with
flowers and fruits. Elephants and lions lived
together with love. Livestock used to gi.ve
milk according to requirements. Land was
always green with crops.

APPENDIX VB
TROUT FISHING DEVELOPMENT FARM
One trout fisheries development farm was
started in the year 1956 at Chergaon with
the following staff:Assistant Warden of Fisheries.
Peon.
Fish Mukkadam.
Fish Watchman.
The site for the farm was selected because
trout fish was in abundance in Pabber and
Andhra. In fact the credit for this project
goes to Mr. Glover, a former Britisher Chief
Conservator of Forests, Punjab. During
1938-39, he introduced trout fish in this part
of the country. To start with, he stocked:
eyed ova of trout fish in a spring water near
Gashwani village five miles away on the
bank of Andhra stream. When these ova
developed into fry, he transferred them to
Andhra Khad by a narrow water char.mel.
Sooner, these little fry developed into big
trout fish in the stream as well as in Pabber.
In initial stages, he took precautions against
any damage to the small ovas. His efforts of
introducing trout fish in the Pabber and
Andhra were a prologue to the establish-'
mellt of the present Farm.
At present, there are four tanks, four nursery ponds and one hatchery room constructe~ by the side of the Government building
bUIlt for the staff of the Fisheries Depart..
ment. Water for the tanks is drawn from
Andhra stream by means of about 100 ft.
long wooden channel. The water is filtered
in the first tank from where it is diverted
to other tanks and Nursery ponds. In the
tanks, big fish are kept whereas in the nur-

sery ponds, small fry are allowed W develop. Both of them are not kept in one tank,
because the big trout fish swallows the small
fry. In between the big tanks and nursery
ponds, is situated a hatchery room where
ova are allowed to hatch out in the hatching trays. Recently, the hatchery room has
been renovated and latest types of equipment has been fitted.
There are at least a dozen of fishing nets
which have been illegally kept by the villagers. The department is taking every Plecaution against illicit fishing. This farm has
been responsible for gradual increase in production of fish. Because of remoteness ot
the area and the small scale prpduction of
trout fish, the question of ma'rketing and
transportation does not arise at present.
Temporary li~enses for angling are given.
The area between Tikkri and Saunda villages along the Pabber is divided into eight
beats for providing good scope for grand
catches because each license-hDlder is directed to fish in different beats. No permanent
license for angling is issued to any body.
The different rates for the temporary licences issued are as follow:Sl'rial

Anglers are not allowed to fish more than
six <trouts per licence, per day. The size of
the catch should not be belpw ten inches.
The anglers are s,upposed to bring their own
fishing equipment (rod) and a good sportsman possesses his own first-aid-box also.
This is used fpr giving aid to the fish if it
is hurt and the catch is less than, 10 inches.
The biggest catch of trout fish in the Pab-

ber is a ten pounder. The record of a trout
in the country is a twenty-five pounder in
Kashmir. Trout bigger than 25 Ibs. do exist.
The trout· fish is very delicious and easy to
handle as it contains only one fish-bone
which can be rempved easily.
In the office of the Assistant Warden of
fisheries, specimens of dead trout and: eyed
ovas are preserved in sealed bottles.

EXTRACT FROM "OPEN AND PENCIL

SKETCHES, BEING THE

JOURNAL OF A TOUR IN INDIA" BY CAPTAIN MUNDY.
The next morning we marched about eight
miles to the village of Rooroo. Our route lay
along the side of a verdant hill, just above
the right bank of the Pahbur, and without
much variety in its elevation. The river, which
is. remarkably rapid, and about forty yards
Wide, meanders fantastically, sometimes in a

single stream, at others in many little rills
through a ~hader, or valley, between tw~
ranges of hIlls. The banks are at this season
c?vered at intervals with very fair crops of
~Ice and oth~r grain. The village of Rooroo
IS more. conSIderable th~n the generality of
mountam-hamlets, and the quantity of level

land in the vicinity affords greater scope .for
c~ltivatio.n. A iarge qpliding iff the,tQwn was
pointed o\.1,t as ~he .pala<:~ of the. Barageh
Ranah. He visited us. in the afternoon,. and
gave us much u~eful informa~ion regard~Iig
our route, affordmg us small hopes of bemg
able to take our hors~s more than two marches farther. One of the Ranah's attendants
brought us a plentiful ,basket of fish ~nd
fruits. He also produced a musk-ball, which
I persuaded him to exchange with me for a
small pen-knife. Its scent was so powerful,
that I could hardly bear i~ among my clothes
in the boxes.
Our next day's journey was about eleven
miles, and was very tidious from the sameness of the scenery and the intense heat. The
road continued partly along the Khader of
the river, and was in parts cut through the
rocky mountain which overhangs its bed.
There are one or two very nervious passages iIi it, wbere the road, scarcely two feet
wide including the coping stone, turns abruptly round jutting buttresses of rock several hundred feet above the stream, which
foams and boils at the foot, Of the perpendicular precipice. The fort of Buttoulee, belonging to the Rajah of Bussahir, a very extensive district, stands on the left bank of
the Pahbur, and is as is usual with the mountain-strongholds-situated on a lofty rock,
standing Qut in lonely grandeur from the
main range.
We passed two or three very considerable
villages on OUI: way, one of which, Bourah,
is very pictu:r;~sque and rQmantic in i~ situation. The method of preserving hay about
h~re is CUrious, ~nd appears tq answer very
well. It is loosely twisted. into long, thick
rop!=s, wh~ch beirig. fastened together at one
e'nd, ,'are thrown: over the fOrk of a tree, the
extremities hanging' doWn, and the apex

pItcHed, just under the' VIllage of. Chergong,
on the banks of a small strea,m, whicn, 'running from the north, falls into the Pahbur,
half a mile hence. The thermometer 84
Here we take leave of the latter river, and
of the good road, (which continues along its
banks towards the more eastern pass of
Borenda,) and, striking off in a more norther:..
ly direction, we are to expect- nothing more
than mountain tracks for the four marches
~etween Chergong and Shattoul Pass. A da~
hU'rkarah reached us to-day with a letter
from Captain KeIineqy,; advising us .not to
att,empt tq carry' forward our: ponies beyond
Chergong-a piece of good advice. cO,mpletely thrown awily, as we h=i\d predete:r;mined to

a letter in these countries is novel enough.

~he letter is tied in a split stick-resembling

the rods llsed in courts of justice for the conveyance of notes over the heads of the multituq.e-and the postman runs along bearing it
aloft; thus' at once declaring his office, and
preserving the dispatch from being soiled.
Oct. 1st. Left Chergong early, and turning
our faces in a northern course, rode, walked,
and, clambered to the village of Dogolee, a
short but very fatiguing march. The pathfor there was no road-led alternately through
rice-fields and along the rocky flank of the
mountain on the left bank of the little torrent above mentioned. Many points were ex" tremely difficult for the passage of horses;
'but sufficiently easy for the high-landers. who
caiT_ied the tents and baggage. After sundry
narrow escapes, the Baron's pony fell over
the side, and became in consequence so timid
that it was not safe to bring him on; he was,
therefore, sent back, with orders to wait at
Khotghur, a large town in Bussahir, until
our arrival there, Both the mules got falls,
but fortunately in a spot where there was no
precipice. The narrow defile through which
we pursued our way, presents very different
features to the comparatively tame scenery'
of our two last marches; the banks of the
stream are beautifully fringed with magnificent alders and other trees of gigantic size;
and the precipitous mountains flanking the
valley on either hand are bristling with fir
forests, from the midst of which the jutting
peaks of black rocks rear their heads.
About half a mile from Dogolee we crossed
the river by a rickety temporary bridge of
rough pines and turf, thrown across two rocks
which confine the stream in a narrow channel. The ponies accomplished the passagewith gr,eat difflc~lty and risk; but the regular sangah having been carried away by the
overflow of the river in the rains, there was
no better means of passing the torrent.
From the river we had a most fatiguin,gascent of a mile and a half, which" as our
ponies and mules were all knocked up, wewere obliged to perform on foot; a necessity
not a little unwelcome to the Baron and myselF" who for the two last days have been
labouring under an indisposition, arising
from our imprudence in bathing in the iced
streams of the glaciers, and from the constant
sudd~p, chang~ of temp~rature to which we'
have been .exposed. We found· our tents
pitched on a pie.ce of plQughed ground near
the hamlet. of Dogolee., Though we are now
witpin thirty miles· of the Pass, the snowy
peaks are ent~rely ,hidde;n from our view; yet
these same glaciers are ,visible from Meerut r
a distance of some twenty marches.

MOUNTAINS VIA GWALIOR, AGRA,
DELm, AND SmmND" BY MAJOR sm WILLIAM LLOYD AND CAPTAIN
ALEXANDER GERARD'S.
16th June Cheergaon, 7 m. 6f.-We changed our rout~ this morning, and went ~y one
that was shorter, better, and more pIcturesque. Leaving Pekka, we descended 14~OO
feet to the Pubbur, which was wreathed wIth
foam. At 3m. 6f. we passed Kundrone ~d
Teekurree. T4e former is a hamlet, prettIly
situated, just above Teekurree; the latter a
\ house belonging to the Rana. Th~ path, ran
occasionally along the rocky. sIdes of the
mountain, overhanging the rIver; but the
footing, in general, was secur~.· The sango
over the Pubbur, at Teekurree, IS a very good
one.
The inhabitants of Cheergaon are very
busy in the poppy-fields collec.ting opium.
The apricots are now almost ripe.
Thermometer; highest 89°, lowest 63°,
Fahr.
17th June, Rooroo, 11m. 2f.-We crossed
over the 'Pub bur by the sango at Mandlee,
which is· ii very disagreeable one, being many
feet above the tremendous torrent, and recrossed at Rooroo by three temporary ones,
over as many branches of the river. The sangos are principally unpleasant, for they are
rarely dangerous, from the swaying of the
spars of' which they are made, and from the
carelessly-arranged platform, which is nothing more than a few ill-nailed planks,
covered with branches and twigs, which are
prevented from being blown away by slabs
of stone of slate.

In this indifferent footing are many gaps,
under which the torrent may be seen, hurled
like a thunderbolt.
We found this road better shaded, and
pleasanter than the one I went by to the
_ Boorendo, which was On the right bank: of
the river. It passed through cultivation almost the whole way.
.
I was informed at Rooroo that Ibrothers usually married the same girl, and that they
divided the off spring· by the gentle law of
primogeniture, the eldest brother taking the
first-born, and so on successively. I am to
sorry to add, that female infanticide is also
known here but it is to be hoped, that since
the British influence extends its authority
over these petty states, it is of secret and are
occurrence.
Rooroo is the residence of the Chief Guru
or Spiritual teacher of Bussheer. He visited
us here on our waY' tb the Boorendo, preceded by some chobdaars or mace-bearers, as a
part of his ecclesiastical state, and very civily
expressed a desire to serve us, and was useful in procuring supplies of grain for our
attendants. He is a fat portly, and goodnatured man. These districts produce two kinds
of rice, one peculiar to the highlands, the
other to the lowlands, besides, wheat and
barley.
We had heavy rain in the evening.
Thermometer; highest 89°, lowest 54~,
Fahr.

APPENDIX XI
Polyandry, as I have before observed, is
frequent, {lnd t]1e men assigned as a reas~m,
that their trading avocations often forcmg
them to be absent for a long period from
tlieir 'homes, it vias requisite that the fem,ales should have more husbands than one.

They also acknowledged that it was not uncommon to sell their children in seasons of
scarGity; indeed most Asiatic nations do the
same under similar circumstances, but not
otherwise.

APPENDIX m
i

Scarcity and famine are of very f.requent
occurrence" from the crops in these elevated
districts ·not ripening. This is chiefly after

severe winters. W~en this· happens, the inhabitants eat venSlOn, sheep, dried fruits
and roots.
'

'90

APPENDICES

The native are in general a hardy and ac·~
tive race, and, as we have often remarked,
.are kind and hospitable.
Domestic cats are common.
Mortar is not used in the construction of
the buildings; in fact, the only limestone I
-saw during the tour, was a formation of
black lime-stone· !between Syree and Semla.
Clay is found in small quantities, but it is
not used. Mica slate is the largest formation
between the Punta Gaut, in the Tayog Purgunnah, and Pekka. Granite is scarce every
where. Compact gneiss is the prevailing rock
'Of the Parent Chain of the Himalay, which
being exposed to the continual vicissitudes
of the weather breaks into fragments, and
forms those stupendous spiry, triangular,
pyramidal, and precipitous gray pinnacles,
which tower above the massive ranges in

barren sublimity. Amongst the lofty moun;tains at the base ot the southern Himalas,
mica, is found, forming rocks of enormous
size and exceeding splendour. We were particularly struck with it on our way up to Jangleeg, where the slope of the valley was radiant with its fragments, like the shattered
remains of some Elfin capital. Veins and
blocks of quartz and feldspar, are abundant.
The uplands in these districts are covered with a rich turfy soil. Tp.ose facing the
S. E. are less precipitous, more thinly wooded, and afford finer pasture tha:n those facing
.the N. and N.W., 'which are usually overhung with large woods, which hide their
steep and torn flanks.
I have. remarked no manufactures, except
-that of the Sooklaat, or coarse woollen cloth,
already mentioned.

APPENDIX XIII
BHOJ BY KHUSHAL CHAND
This fair was held at village Guma twelve
miles away f.rom Rohru from 26th November,
1964 to 28th November, 1964 for three days.
, There is a jeepable road upio Chirgaon and
from there one has to proceed on foot. It is
inhabited by Brahmins and Kolies. Their occupation is agriculture. The Kolies have a
separate section about 100 yards away from
where the Brahmins live. There are three
temples in this village. One temple is of Devta
Beinsar, second is of Parshu Ram and third
of Kilbaru Devta. Of these temples, the first
two are situated in the sections of Brahmins
and the third one of Kilbaru Devta is situated in the Muhallah of Kolies. Devta "Beinsar
is considered the chief deity of the village.
A Government Primary School is also functioning there.
In ancient times a Brahmin named Guman
Dev came on excursion from 'Kashi' and
settled permanently. As he had come only
for travel (Ghumnay-phirnay) so he named
the village 'Guma'.
Devta Beinsar·
Later on Gumman Dev meditated on
Chansher mountain. It was because of his
meditations that three Devtas 'Bahishar',
'Beinsar' and 'Bana Shira' who liv-ed over the
.mountain started from there. Bahishar Devta
left for Garhwal in Uttar Pradesh. Devta
Beinsar and Devta Bana Shira came on bet
that who~so-ever dances first in a ground of
village Guma shall be considered the eldeI1
deity. All the three Devtas parted from Chan'sher mountain. While Banashira Devta was

on his way to Guma village he came across
some ~olies. He was overtaken by them and
they started gambling. Beinsar Devta came
straight to Guma village and organised a
dance (Bishnu fair) there in a ground where
the present temple of Beinsar Devta stands.
When Devta Banashira came to know this he
got angry and halted the dance. Beinsar
Devta then gave him his own way and admitted his supremacy. Then Banashira Devta
came to Guma village and lived alongwith
Beinsar Devta. Since then the idol of Banashira Devta is placed at the head of 'rath' and
below him is kept the idol of Beinsar Devta.
However the temple is famous by the name
of Beinsar Devta temple. The present temple
has been built by the people about 40 years
ago. The idol of Banashira Devta is made 0:1)
silver and· that of Beinsar Devta is made of
brass. The decendants of Gumman Dev are
the priests and Malis of this deity tiW today.
KiIbaru Devta
Kilbaru Devta has its temples in different
parts of Rohru Tehsil. It has been adopted
by the scheduled castes 'Kolies'. It is said
that actually the deity belongs to village
'Kilba' in District Kinnaur. There it is worshipped and propitiated by the upper castes.
From there it also migrated to Rohru Tehsil
and somehow became untouchable. Then it
was adopted by the Kolies and they built its
temples at various places in their localities.
Its idols are made of silver which are of similar shapes at different temples. These

APPENDICES'

idols are decorated with cloth pieces. The
silver idols of Bishnu and Brass idols of
Panch Birs mou.nted on iron sticks also, accompany the Kilbaru Devta. The Kolies are
the priests and Malies of this Devta. People
of: upper castes of the particular village
where KHbaru's temple stands also bow j;o
this deity and make offerings to him.

Bhoj Fair/Festival
Bhoj is the name of a feast arranged by
the Kolies of a village at their temple of Kllbaru Devta on his behalf to entertain the fellow Kolies hav,ing Kilbaru Devtas and living
at other villages and vice versa. The Devtas
\ and people of upper castes of other villagers
except the village where it is held do not
make any contributipns in its celebration.
The Kolies also do not entertain the people
of upper castes living outside their own village. It is not held regularly as it is very expensiVe and people cannot afford it too often.
It is held only when the people are sure that
they have a good crop and are in a position
to bear its expenses. The purpose of its holding is to strengthen the solidarity and friendship amongst the people of one caste living
at different places.
Date of the fair
One year before the Bhoj fair the Mali or
Kilbaru Devta forecasts that his Bhoj should
be arranged for the ,next year. The people
start grinding wheat and husking rice 6
months ahead of Bhoj. One month before the
chief deity of the village Le. Devta Beinsar
in Guma village gives particular dates for
holding the fair. Kilbaru Devta sends invitations to the other Kilbaru Devtas whom he
wants to invite for the Bhoj through'messangers four days before the fair/festival. The
number of Kilbaru Devtas to be invited depends upon the capacity of the inhabitants of
that particular village to feast them. The Koli
inhabitants make mutual contributions of
wheat flour, oil, rice, and· coins amongst
themselves and gain some help from the
Devta's temples and other upper caste inhabitants of the village.
Bhoj fair at Guma

Arrangements
Arrangement for this fair was done collectively by the Kolies and Priests, Malies and
trustees of Kilbaru and Beinsar Devta of
Guma village. Invitations were sent to four
Kilbaru Devtas of Kharshali, Larot, Tikkari
and Gar Villages. There is no limit to the
number of persons accompanying .each deity.
All the Koli inhabitants males, females and

91

children of the particular village can accompany the Kilbaru Devta. All of them are receiv~d as guests by the inviting deity and are
prOVIded food provisions till the fair lasts.
Programme and recreation.
On 26th .November, 1964 the Koli villagers
of Guma VIllage prepared halwa, allu-ki-sabzi
and pakwans during day time to be served
to the Kol~ invitees in the evening. Dry ration
was supphed to the, people of Beinsar Devta.
At 4. P. M. the Beinsar Devta mounted on
a wooden frame called 'Rath' was taken out
of his temple to a ground in front of the
temple with the beating of Dhols, Nagaras
Bhana, Kansar and Ransingas. The Devt~
was carried by two persons on their shoulders (one in, front and the other behind). The
Kilbaru Devta of Guma village was also
taken out alongwith the idols of Bishnu and
Panch Birs from his temple in Kolis Muhallah and was brought to meet the Beinsar
Devta in the ground. The main idol of Kilbaru De.vta was carried over by the Mali on
his head. The idols of 'Bishnu' and Birs were
carried by their respecive MaBes in their
hands. This deity was brought ceremonially
with the beating of musical instruments. On
reaching in the ground the Mali carrying Kilbaru Devta over his head shivered, came ip
action and then came near the Rath of Beinsar Devta the chief deity. From there he
bowed before him several times showing that
the Kilbaru Devta was paying respects to the
chief deity. Being untouchable he did not
touch the 'Rath' of the chief deity. Then he
went back and stood at one side of the
ground. He was given a hand full of barley
flour which he threw in the air. Then turn by
turn or together as the case may be th~ invited deities came with the beating of musical
instruments. First, all of them bowed before
the chief deity and afterwards the Kilbaru
devtas carried over their heads by the Malies
were joined together showing that they were
meeting each other. Then they drew back and
stood at one side of the ground. After this the
other Malies carrying the idols of Bishnu and
Panch Birs also shivered and came in action.
They too bowed before the chief deity and
were given barley flour to be thrown in the
air. Then they met with the respective MaHes
of other Kilbaru Devtas. After their meeting
all of them came together before the chief
deity with iron arrows in their hands. They
bowed before him again and then pierced the
arrows through their cheeks, and middle of
noses. Some of them pierced through one
cheek from inside the mouth and some
through both the cheeks from one and to the
other. It is said that by this act the Kilbaru
Devta makes his power visible upon the masses as the Malies feel no pain while inserting

92

the arrows and not even a single drop of blood
drips out during the course and afterwards.
When all the Malies had inserted the arrow
they again bowed before the chief deity and
their own deities and then danced with the
tune of musical instruments. After a short
while the procession proceeded from the
ground towards the temple of Kilbaru Devtao In the way the Beiusar Devta stayed in a
field and the other Devtas went to Kilbaru
Devta's temple in the Kolies section. There
they stayed for the night. The arrows were
taken out by the Malies themselves and il1en

feel cold at night. The different parties of

stayed at the houses of upper caste Brahmins with whom they had got some acquaintance or had accompanied a person of their
village who was known there.
After having their meals the people' again
came in the fields and held dances (Nati and
Mala) during the whole night. The Kolies
held separate dances and the upper caste
people held their ow,n separate dances as
the upper caste people do not dance with the
scheduled castes. The ladies and gents d::mced together.
In the morning of 27th November, all the

that no ghost (Bhoot Prait) may be able to

Devtas came in action and pierced iron arrows through their noses and cheeks. A few
"Mashals" were burnt there and a lamb was

The people started Naties.
Naties were of two types. One was a Nati
in which musical instruments were beaten
and the people danced at the tune of music
with swords in one hand and their sheaths
in the other. They did not sing any songs.
Only gents took part in that dance. In the
second type of dance (Nati) the males and
females danced jointly. There was no playing of musical instruments. But first--two men
sang a line of some song and then two women,
repeated that line. Then the man would sing
the second line of the song and the women
would repeat that. Thus the whole song would
be sung. After completion of this the next
song was started. The naties continued till
, the evening. This was the peak day of the
fair.
In the evening the parties concerned again
started cooking their evening meals. Rice and
meat was given to them to be cooked. At sun
set the Kilbaru Devtas were again taken back
to the Kilbaru Devtas temple. There they
were worshipped and kept for the night.
After having the evening meal the people
again gathered in the fields and held dances
during the whole night.

On this day particular type of songs locally called 'Lamans' were sung. It is sort of
singing co.mp,etition putting questions and
answers gIvmg between the males and females. In this contest the ladies and gents
(two or more then two) divide themselves in
two parts (ladies on, the one side and ge.nts on
the other side). One section sits at the brim
of one field and the other at the brim of another field. T:hey remain about 10 to 16 yards
away from each other so that one section can
fully recognise the voice of another section.
One section raises questions in the song and
the other one gives replies there to. This
sort of contest is said to be very familiar in
the illaqua and continues for hours together.

Diet arrangements
Special diet arrangements were made for

Dress
Mates-They usually put on 'Chapkan',
Pyjama, Shirt, Bushehri Cap and Shoes. The
Malies of Devtas wore this dress but while
under the influence of their deity they wore
a white cloth sheet (Janew) round one shoulder which covered half the waist. It is said
that by wearing it they be"Come sacred and
the ghosts (bhoot prait) cannot influence
them. It is not compulsory for the Brahmin
and Rajput Malies to wear this cloth sheet
because they already have the sacred, thread.
It is very essential for the Males of Scheduled
Castes because they have no custom to put
on a sacred thread. They carry a branch of
'Bhekhar' thorn (having leaves and thorns)
in one hand while shivering in a trance.
This too is do,ne to keep away the ghosts.
Females-They generally wear Dhatu or
round caps surrounded by a piece of cloth
"Talla", Chapkan, Shirt, Rebdar Pyjama and
